#3054. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 13: Kind of a Big Jump

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates rose enough today that you might be feeling some sticker shock.

Let's just say that mortgage rates are looking appropriately scary for Friday the 13th.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.26% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 17 basis points higher than yesterday and 28 basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This morning, the Bureau of Labor Statistics released the Producer Price Index for October, which tracks the change in prices that U.S. producers are able to get for their products. This is one way to measure inflation on the market end, rather than the consumer end.

The results were mixed. While prices didn’t rise quite as high as economists anticipated month-over-month (0.3% vs. 0.4%), year-over-year price growth did reach 6.5% — the largest increase in four years. This points to even higher costs for consumers coming in the near future.

Investors are convinced that the Federal Reserve will raise overnight borrowing rates again in December to pull inflation down. And higher overnight borrowing rates — called the federal funds rate, which is what banks pay to fund products like mortgages — usually lead to higher mortgage rates.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.76% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

#3052. The Cognitive Enterprise In 2027: Four Priorities For AI Maturity

Casey Kindiger is a technology entrepreneur and CEO of Droma (formerly Grokstream).

​When a customer reports a service disruption, different parts of the business often see different problems. Operations sees an infrastructure alert, finance sees a billing exception, and account management sees a renewal at risk. Each team may have AI tools to interpret its information, yet connecting those perspectives still requires people to exchange messages, reconcile records and find someone who remembers a similar incident. AI is accelerating individual tasks, but it is also exposing a fundamental weakness in how enterprises operate: Intelligence remains fragmented, even as the technology becomes more capable.

As organizations prepare for 2027, closing that gap should become a leadership priority. A recent McKinsey article, citing its latest state of AI survey, reports that 80% of respondents say AI has improved their individual productivity, while only 37% report a positive impact on earnings before interest and taxes (EBIT). That gap illustrates the challenge ahead: Translating local improvements into better business outcomes requires changes to how information, decisions and responsibility move across the enterprise.

Having spent much of my career building operations automation and AI systems, I see four priorities for measurable AI maturity in 2027:

The cognitive enterprise offers a useful framework for this transition. The concept predates generative AI; IBM described it in its 2019 annual report as an organization informed by data and AI and equipped to adapt. Generative AI now makes institutional knowledge more accessible, while agentic AI can pursue objectives through multiple steps and systems. What is different now is the possibility of a more explicit cognitive architecture: systems that connect perception, institutional memory, judgment, governed action and learning across the enterprise.

These capabilities bring the vision closer, but their usefulness depends on the information and authority available to them. An agent cannot reliably resolve a customer issue when account records disagree, procedures are outdated or responsibility for approving a remedy is unclear.

After years of building businesses around IT operations and AI-driven automation, I’ve learned that identifying a problem is rarely the hardest part. The greater challenge is connecting what the organization knows to the decisions it needs to make—and ensuring those decisions lead to meaningful outcomes.

The organization also needs to connect technical evidence with business context and carry that understanding across teams as they decide what to do. Otherwise, each department can become more efficient while the overall process remains slow, inconsistent and dependent on a few experienced employees.

A modern cognitive enterprise connects six capabilities: sensing changing conditions, retrieving institutional knowledge, evaluating possible responses, governing authority, acting within defined boundaries and learning from verified outcomes. Together, they support a continuous process in which new information informs decisions and the results improve subsequent work. This requires more than access to documents. Institutional knowledge includes why a previous response succeeded, which assumptions proved wrong and when an exception was justified. Making that context available helps teams evaluate unfamiliar situations without reconstructing every decision from scratch.

In the service-disruption example, a connected workflow could associate technical symptoms with affected customers, retrieve previous remedies, assess service commitments and recommend a coordinated response. A reversible technical action might proceed automatically, while a consequential service change would require approval. Customer-facing teams could receive the same verified account of what happened and what comes next. The resulting value includes less repeated investigation, fewer contradictory recommendations and shorter delays between identifying a problem and resolving its business consequences. Recording the outcome also gives the organization a stronger basis for responding when conditions change again.

As AI systems gain the ability to act across systems, governance becomes part of the architecture that makes useful autonomy possible, not a control layer added afterward. Each workflow needs an accountable owner, clear limits on automated action and a record of the evidence supporting consequential decisions. Access to information should not automatically confer authority to change a system or make a customer commitment. Human reviewers need sufficient context to evaluate recommendations, along with practical ways to pause, redirect or reverse execution.

My view, informed by work in operational AI, is that broader responsibility should follow demonstrated performance on the decisions a system will actually handle. A system that identifies missing evidence and requests assistance can be more useful than one that produces a confident response to every situation. Learning requires comparable discipline: Teams should verify an action’s effects before treating it as a precedent. Otherwise, an apparent success can reinforce a flawed assumption or conceal costs transferred to another department.

Leaders can begin with one workflow that crosses departmental boundaries and has a measurable consequence, such as service restoration, customer onboarding or order exception handling. Map where work stalls, where employees repeatedly search for information and where handoffs lose context. Establish baseline measures for completion time, rework, errors and customer impact, then identify the knowledge needed to improve those results. That foundation should include authoritative sources, consistent definitions and named owners responsible for keeping information current. Employees who manage exceptions should help shape the design because their experience often reveals gaps in formal procedures.

Before enabling automated action, define what AI may recommend, what it may execute and when it must escalate. Evaluate results across the complete workflow, including downstream workload and oversight costs, before extending the approach. Teams can then extend the same information sources, decision rules and safeguards to related workflows, allowing enterprise intelligence to develop incrementally. The more important measure is whether each resolved case improves the starting point for the next one. AI maturity should increase not only execution speed but learning velocity.

In 2027, AI maturity won’t be measured by the number of agents deployed or tasks automated. It will be measured by whether the enterprise becomes more capable because of the work it has already done—turning experience into better judgment, safer action and faster learning.

#3045. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#3044. Rethinking How Teams Plan, Make Decisions And Work Together

Deniz Gezgin is CEO of Eightpoint, a global technology company building & scaling consumer digital products across mobile & desktop.

​When I became CEO of Eightpoint in July 2024, I joined a company with experienced teams and strong technical capabilities across desktop and mobile software. I saw an opportunity to connect that expertise more closely and build a company where teams could make decisions with a clearer understanding of the broader business.

We’ve spent much of the past two years working through that change. Bringing different functions together around shared priorities has required us to revisit how information moves through the organization and how decisions are made.

That experience has shaped my perspective on AI. As new tools make development, analysis and testing more efficient, the way a company operates becomes increasingly important.

One of the first things I noticed was how much time could be lost between capable teams working toward different priorities. Engineering, product, analytics and marketing each had valuable expertise. When information stayed within individual departments, teams sometimes approached similar problems independently or made decisions without the full context.

We’ve worked to bring shared capabilities—including data, platform and design—closer to our product teams. This gives people access to expertise across the company and helps reduce duplicated effort.

I believe leaders introducing AI should pay close attention to these existing workflows. Understanding where decisions slow down can reveal opportunities to improve how the organization works as a whole.

Annual and quarterly planning remain useful for establishing direction and allocating resources. At the same time, product teams often receive meaningful feedback long before the next formal planning review.

I’ve become more interested in organizing execution around shorter learning cycles while keeping longer-term business priorities consistent. A team can work toward an annual objective while using two-week sprints to evaluate specific assumptions, make improvements and determine where to focus next. Our product development approach emphasizes defining a problem, testing an idea and using the results to guide additional investment.

There are limits to how frequently a company can change direction. Teams need enough stability to complete meaningful work, and some decisions require more time to evaluate.

As a CEO, I’ve learned that the way leaders make decisions has a significant influence on how quickly the rest of the company can move. During periods of change, it’s natural to want greater visibility into what’s happening across the business. I’ve had to think carefully about where my involvement is useful and where it can slow people down.

We’ve been working toward clearer ownership across our teams. I want leaders to understand the outcomes they’re responsible for, have access to the information they need and feel confident making decisions within their areas. That comes with expectations around accountability. People need to explain their reasoning, make results visible and raise issues early enough for the organization to respond.

When an experiment produces an unexpected result, I want to understand how the team approached the decision and what it recommends doing next.

AI’s creating opportunities for teams to produce more work in less time. As that happens, I think leaders need to become more thoughtful about how they evaluate progress. Traditional measures such as features delivered, campaigns launched and projects completed remain useful, but they provide only part of the picture.

I’m interested in how quickly teams can validate assumptions, identify problems and apply what they’ve learned.

In consumer software, the results of an experiment can be difficult to interpret. A change might improve engagement among one group of users while having little effect elsewhere. Teams need time to understand those differences and determine whether the improvement supports the broader product.

This requires a culture where people can discuss disappointing results openly. I’ve found that the way leadership responds in those moments has a lasting influence on how teams approach future decisions.

Culture becomes visible in the everyday decisions people make. One of our priorities has been creating stronger connections between the teams responsible for developing products, understanding users and growing the business.

For example, bringing Spigot and Eightpoint Technologies together under the Eightpoint brand gave us a shared identity. The more involved work has been making that identity meaningful through clearer priorities, stronger ownership and better collaboration.

I’ve learned that encouraging people to work together only goes so far. They also need shared information, aligned objectives and a clear understanding of who’s responsible for moving decisions forward. The relationships between people and the trust required to make decisions together still need attention from leadership.

I’m encouraged by the practical ways AI is already supporting our work. Our desktop team is using AI-assisted tools for browser upgrades and automated testing, with human approval remaining part of the development and release process.

As these capabilities develop, I expect companies will have more opportunities to experiment and respond to changing user needs. Leaders will need to think carefully about how they organize teams, allocate resources and create room for learning.

I’m proud of the progress we’ve made, particularly the broader ownership our leaders have taken and the way teams are becoming more connected.

For me, the opportunity ahead is to build a company that can keep improving its products, its decisions and the way people work together. The progress we make will depend on how thoughtfully we put those capabilities to work.

#3042. Try These Ways to Use Less Stuff (and Save More Money)

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Personal finance writers and editors share their favorite ways to be resourceful.

Have you ever thrown away groceries that spoiled before you got around to using them? Or bought a new jacket despite already having half a dozen in your closet?

It’s easy to let things go to waste and accumulate more than we need.

Use Less Stuff Day, which lands on Nov. 19 this year, encourages us to rethink our consumption habits and make more mindful, environmentally friendly choices.

That might mean squeezing every last drop out of what you’ve got, finding creative ways to reuse things or simply buying less.

I keep a couple of simple tricks up my sleeve to extend the life of my family’s clothing: OxiClean soaks and basic sewing skills. You’d be surprised by how many things you can salvage with a little cleaning and mending.

I asked my fellow Nerds to share some of the ways they reduce waste and unnecessary spending. Maybe you’ll pick up an idea or two.

Pamela de la Fuente, managing editor: My husband grew up with a “make what’s in the house” kinda family. I didn’t. But this weekend while looking for spaghetti I found some crispy rice cereal, and I knew we had marshmallows from a bonfire a few weeks ago so I said, “I’m going to make crispy rice treats with this for the kids and get rid of this cereal.” (Surprisingly, it was not stale.) I still had cereal left, so I added it to some chocolate protein bars I love to make and my son said, “These are 9 out of 10. Usually they are 7 out of 10!”

Karen Gaudette Brewer, head of content: I’m a big “make big batches of food and freeze it” person, so I can drive everyone nuts reminding them that we really do have food at home. That freezer also holds grated lemon zest and cubes of those last little bits of chicken broth and wine so that I can easily make pan sauces and not waste that last half cup of goodness.

Jessica Cano, content coordinator: I save the tops from strawberries after cutting them for my kids. We use some to make strawberry balsamic vinegar, some to make strawberry syrup and the rest go in the freezer to be used for smoothies. Keeps me from having to buy frozen fruit and multiple vinegars.

Pantry shopping can result in less food in the trash and fewer trips to the grocery store. It can also make experimenting with new recipes a little more exciting. Take a page from Managing Editor Athena Cocoves, who says, “Sometimes we pretend we're on ‘Chopped.’”

Ryan Brady, lead writer: Rotating toys with our kids has helped them get more enjoyment out of the same toys versus constantly buying new toys. We put a bunch of toys they're bored with in a bin, and months later, take one out and bam — it's like a new toy all over again!

Kim Lowe, head of content: I invested a whole $12 in a lint remover last winter that transformed all my old sweaters into almost new!

Laura McMullen, editor: Almost all of my kids’ clothes are thrifted or hand-me-downs. My friend group of parents has kids from age 2 to 7, all of whom are about six months apart. We’ve developed a hearty clothing exchange. It’s fun to see my friends' kids wear hand-me-downs that we got from my brother’s family almost 10 years ago.

Look for other ways to salvage your belongings or get them secondhand. That might include watching a vacuum repair tutorial on YouTube, borrowing books from the library or purchasing used tools from Facebook Marketplace. The goal: Keep more stuff in circulation and out of landfills.

Abby Doyle, lead writer: Composting isn’t as fussy as it seems. Once you get into it, you start seeing would-be waste as stuff to feed your new backyard buddy. Junk mail, paper bags, slimy spring mix … it’s all food for my “pet dirt.” And my garden has legit never looked better.

Yuliya Goldshteyn, editor: Instead of throwing away kid art, I reuse it as wrapping paper throughout the year. Bespoke gift wrapping for everyone! I haven’t bought gift wrap for five years now.

Caitlin Constantine, editor: I repurpose glass jars and plastic containers. It always seemed like such a pity to throw perfectly good containers into the recycling after one use, so now I reuse them before I recycle them.

Before you toss something, ask yourself, “Is there another way I can use this?” An old T-shirt makes a good cleaning rag, for example.

Being resourceful often means getting as much mileage as possible out of what you have.

#3041. What Is a Business Development Company? 2 BDC Stocks to Buy for Income.

These two BDCs deliver yields of 7.6% and 9.8%, respectively.

Many investors are familiar with REITs -- or real estate investment trusts -- and their ability to deliver high-yielding dividends.

Business development companies, or BDCs, are not as well known to the average investor, but they essentially do the same thing: deliver huge dividends.

BDCs were created by federal law in 1980 to funnel public investment into small- and mid-sized companies that don't have the same access to private capital as larger firms. Like REITs, BDCs are required by law to distribute 90% of their taxable income as dividends in exchange for certain tax breaks.

Also like REITs, BDCs tend to provide investors with substantial income. High-yield dividends are their main benefit for investors, but some also deliver decent returns.

Here are two of the best BDC stocks to buy right now.

Main Street Capital (MAIN +0.30%) is pretty much the gold standard among BDCs. Since it went public in 2007, Main Street Capital has never reduced its dividend, raising its monthly payout annually for 19 straight years -- and counting (it also pays out a semiannual supplemental dividend that it did suspend in 2020 during the COVID-19 pandemic). Earlier this month, the company approved a dividend increase for the first three months of 2027, marking the 20th straight year of regular dividend increases.

Main Street Capital boosted its dividend for January, February, and March 2027 to $0.27 per share, up from the current $0.265 per share.

The other thing that makes Main Street Capital stand out is that it pays a monthly dividend, rather than quarterly. The more frequent payouts give investors a steady flow of income. And the dividend payouts come at a high yield of 7.66% (when including the semiannual supplemental dividends), which is 7 times higher than the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.40%)average.

Main Street Capital has been so consistent in raising its dividend because of its model, which features two primary benefits. One, it is internally managed, so it handles loans in-house rather than using third-party asset managers. That gives it more direct control over its investments, but also reduces the fees it would pay out to a third party.

Two, it holds an equity stake in most of the lower-middle-market investments it makes. So, it profits from the companies it invests in through its equity stake. That makes it, perhaps, much more selective and focused on growing its investments in these LMM companies.

This has allowed it to not only sustain its dividend but also perform relatively well from a capital appreciation perspective. Since it went public in October 2007, Main Street Capital stock has averaged a 7.2% annualized return, which is solid for a BDC. But if you reinvested the dividends, that average annualized return jumps to 16.6%.

So, the high-yield dividend puts money in your pocket every month, and if you choose to reinvest it, it has a track record of generating solid returns.

Ares Capital (ARCC +0.31%) is another excellent BDC that pays an even higher yield than Main Street Capital, but it does not have the same degree of sustainability as Main Street.

Ares is the largest publicly traded BDC, with a market cap of around $14 billion. It invests in larger companies than Main Street, primarily middle-market firms. It focuses on high‑quality companies that are market leaders with sturdy cash flows, experienced management, and differentiated business models.

The stock pays a super-high dividend yield of 9.88%. It's different from Main Street in that it pays its yield quarterly, rather than monthly. It also doesn't have the same streak of raising its dividend. However, it has maintained its dividend at $0.48 per share for the past four straight years, since late 2022.

And before that, it hadn't lowered its quarterly dividend since 2009. It has also periodically paid out supplemental dividends over that time.

Ares Capital stock has averaged just a 2.2% return over the past 10 years, but with the dividend reinvested, that increases to a 12.4% average annualized 10-year return.

In an uncertain market for BDCs, with borrowers in general facing economic challenges, these two stand out for their rigorous lending standards, conservative underwriting, and stability. But if one stands out as the better option for investors, it is Main Street Capital.

#3040. Feeling Is The Human Superpower AI May Never Learn

​Imagine an old voicemail from someone you have lost. You know every word. You listen anyway.

An AI might eventually explain every mechanism behind that attachment. It could recognize the voice, reconstruct the relationship and predict your response. None of that would, by itself, establish that it knows what it is to miss someone.

Humans are feeling machines that can think. AIs are thinking machines that try to feel.

The second half describes an engineering aspiration, not an established inner life. Intelligence and subjective experience are not interchangeable concepts.

My argument is that feeling is our deepest power: the capacity for existence to matter to us. It should never become something we apologize for as machines grow more capable.

The human half of that contrast owes a debt to neuroscientist Antonio Damasio. His account locates feelings in the relationship between the nervous system and the body it helps regulate. Hunger, pain, comfort and attachment belong to a biological history shaped by evolution. They sustain life, rather than merely decorating our thoughts.

Millions of years stand behind this inheritance. Yet it takes on a particular history in each of us: This place becomes home; this person becomes irreplaceable. I see feeling as one of evolution’s most extraordinary achievements—life becoming significant to itself.

A recent philosophical account of consciousness develops a related argument: Experience may depend on being a subject for whom things can genuinely go well or badly. On this view, processing information is insufficient without the internal organization that makes outcomes matter to the system itself.

This changes how we might regard our evolutionary origins. Explaining love through the history of survival and reproduction does not establish that love is merely a reproductive strategy. An account of where something comes from cannot, on its own, decide what it is worth.

Nor does calling feeling a superpower mean our feelings are always right. Fear can imprison us; affection can distort judgment. Reason helps us examine both. The argument is against confusing freedom from error with freedom from caring.

In a September 2026 paper, Geoffrey Hinton and his coauthors examine how automating AI research could create an accelerating cycle: Capable systems help build still more capable successors. An intelligence explosion is a possibility they take seriously, not an established outcome.

Even if it happens, the question of feeling survives. Greater ability to explain the world does not, by itself, establish that a machine experiences being in it.

Physical AI sharpens the distinction. Robotic touch sensors can register pressure and heat. Whether holding someone’s hand brings comfort is another question.

Some researchers argue that consciousness may depend on properties of living systems, rather than computation alone. Others allow that artificial systems could acquire the organization and relationships needed for experience. The issue remains unsettled.

AI may never learn to feel simply by becoming better at thinking. We should take that possibility seriously instead of assuming that an inner life arrives automatically with sufficient scale.

And should machines eventually feel, our feelings would not become obsolete. Another being’s capacity to love does not diminish yours. Human worth should never have depended on possessing a capability nobody else could acquire.

Perhaps feeling is a kind of password to an interior dimension of existence. I mean this as a philosophical image, not a claim about hidden physics: Through feeling, the universe becomes something we inhabit intimately.

We need not invoke another world to recognize how extraordinary that is. Within this one, we can be moved by a voice, overwhelmed by beauty or changed by someone else’s suffering.

Consider time. An hour waiting for someone carries something a clock cannot record: hope that they will arrive. Perhaps part of our wish for immortality comes from wanting that encounter with existence to continue. We still want to feel more.

For those of us building AI, this changes what progress should mean.

A tool that saves an hour and immediately fills it with more work has not necessarily enriched a life. An assistant that recognizes loneliness should help strengthen a person’s relationships, not make the product harder to leave. Emotional understanding should serve the person who feels, rather than make that person easier to influence.

The point of building more capable machines should be to give people more room to live.

That asks something of us, too. We should resist using intelligence to insulate ourselves from every uncertainty. Preventable danger deserves safeguards. But a relationship whose future is unwritten, an unfamiliar idea or a discovery that unsettles us is not merely a prediction problem waiting to be solved.

Curiosity means remaining available to what we have not yet experienced. We can welcome a universe that becomes more intelligible without demanding that it stop surprising us.

Perhaps this is what each generation can leave the next: better ways to understand the world, and more freedom to encounter it. Future artificial minds may join that inheritance. They need not become the standard against which every human life is judged.

We do not have to remain the most intelligent beings imaginable for our lives to matter.

The voicemail is not precious because we cannot explain it. It is precious because we still want to hear the person’s voice.

Forbes Technology is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3038. Orion Group: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Prime contractor status on Navy contracts positions Orion for direct access to federal task orders, a potential structural advantage beyond the pending bid pipeline.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 28, 2026, Orion Group (ORN -1.05%) reported that its pending bid pipeline reached $2 billion. For long-term investors, expanding contract opportunities in marine construction and mission-critical concrete work signal potential future revenue growth despite delayed customer project awards. Three key takeaways stand out for long-term investors.

Project owners are taking longer to award contracts, but Orion Group's underlying bid volume is growing rapidly. Its active bids awaiting decisions grew from $1 billion at the start of the year to $2 billion, split evenly between marine construction and concrete segments.

Management secured prime contractor spots on two major U.S. Navy Multiple Award Construction Contracts, or MACCs, which are multiyear federal vehicles that let selected builders bid directly on upcoming task orders. Securing prime status gives the business early positioning on defense infrastructure investments in the Pacific Northwest.

Orion Group expanded its concrete platform into Florida and Arizona to reduce reliance on its traditional Texas market. Broadening its footprint insulates operations against regional weather disruptions and opens new revenue streams in mission-critical facilities like data centers and cold storage.

Bidding activity across marine and concrete segments is accelerating, and results are tracking toward the upper end of full-year 2026 guidance. Geographic diversification and prime access to federal contract vehicles provide structural advantages, while execution discipline and working capital conversion remain key operational focus areas.

Capturing these large infrastructure opportunities depends on turning submitted bids into funded backlog while maintaining margin discipline across both operating divisions.

#3036. Stock Market Today, Nov. 13: Nvidia Slides After HSBC Downgrades Stock to Hold on CEO Uncertainty

On Nov. 13, 2026, Nvidia shares faced a fresh HSBC downgrade as investors weighed leadership changes and an upcoming earnings call.

Nvidia (NVDA -2.01%), an AI accelerator, graphics processor, and data-center networking solutions provider, closed at $162.85, down 2.01%. HSBC downgraded the stock to hold, and investors are watching the earnings call on Nov. 17.Trading volume reached 360.7M shares, coming in about 15% above its three-month average of 144.3M shares. Nvidia IPO'd in 1999 and has grown 396,931% since going public.

The S&P 500 (SNPINDEX: ^GSPC) fell 0.41% to 7,561, and the Nasdaq Composite (NASDAQINDEX: ^IXIC) dropped 0.90% to 25,139. Among semiconductor peers, Advanced Micro Devices closed at $534.06, down 8.75%, and Broadcom closed at $347.01, down 4.76%, as AI hardware names faced a weaker session.

The fallout from former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation continues amid HSBC's downgrade.

HSBC analyst Frank Lee not only downgraded the chip stock to a hold but also cut its price target from $375 per share to $170, just 4% above Friday's closing price. Lee believes the stock will struggle until Nvidia names a permanent CEO.

However, the second reason for the HSBC downgrade is likely less obvious to most investors. About 11% of Nvidia's revenue over the last six quarters came from outside the U.S. and Taiwan. Lee believes these revenue streams could face more stringent export rules, further pressuring the company's earnings.

Regardless of that possible concern, investors and analysts alike seem to be writing off Nvidia stock. Consequently, all eyes are likely to be on Nvidia's upcoming earnings report for the third quarter of fiscal 2027. That takes place on Nov. 17 and will be the first without Jensen Huang.

Although the company should continue to deliver rapid revenue and earnings growth, investors will more likely focus on the conference call and hints about the company's future. Amid that uncertainty and the dramatically lower price target, current shareholders are likely best off following HSBC's advice and treating this as a hold until further information comes to light.

#3034. First Commonwealth: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Recurring federal energy tax credits could reduce the bank's effective tax rate by 1 to 2 percentage points annually.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 28, 2026, First Commonwealth (FCF +0.05%) reported core earnings per share of $0.45. For long-term investors, the bank's ability to trim deposit costs while expanding loan originations shows the underlying operational durability of its community franchise.

Management expects its deposit beta, the percentage of central bank interest rate changes passed through to depositors, to remain near historical levels of 25% on non-maturity accounts without fixed expiration dates. Paying market rates on core accounts keeps deposit levels steady while protecting net interest margin, the gap between what the bank earns on loans and pays on deposits.

First Commonwealth purchased over $50 million in federal renewable energy tax credits to offset tax liabilities and fund credit loss provisions. Settling into an annual rhythm of credit purchases could reduce the bank's effective tax rate by 1 to 2 percentage points.

The bank repurchased $88 million of stock during the quarter to optimize its tangible common ratio, a key measure of physical capital strength. Board authorization of an additional $25 million buyback program allows management to continue repurchasing shares from retained earnings.

Loan originations are rising across consumer and commercial categories as loan payoffs slow, driving loan volume higher. First Commonwealth relies on its regional branch footprint across Western Pennsylvania and Ohio alongside 35 fintech partnerships, or technology start-up agreements, to maintain pricing discipline and improve operating efficiency.

Long-term expansion depends on keeping loan growth in the mid-single digits while keeping loan-to-deposit ratios in the low 90% range. Management is focused on expanding technology investments, improving credit performance, and controlling expenses to build durable shareholder value.

#3031. Top Wall Street analysts believe in the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East and soaring bond yields weighed on global markets this past week. And investors are closely assessing the valuations of artificial intelligence stocks amid concerns about the sustainability of high spending on AI infrastructure.

Despite those continued challenges, top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the prospects of several stocks due to their long-run growth potential and exposure to lucrative business lines, including AI.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD) is this week's first stock pick. The company is scheduled to announce fiscal second-quarter results on August 26.

Ahead of Q2 FY27 earnings, Truist analyst Junaid Siddiqui reiterated a buy rating on CrowdStrike stock and raised his price target to $245 from $187.50. Following CrowdStrike's impressive fiscal first-quarter performance and guidance raise, Siddiqui expects another strong quarter.

The 5-star analyst's optimism is backed by continued strength in Falcon Flex adoption and encouraging large deal activity. Siddiqui also cited continued momentum across emerging products. With the FY27 outlook assuming persistent strength in CrowdStrike's net new annual recurring revenue (ARR), Siddiqui is more focused on the longevity of key growth catalysts such as Falcon Flex adoption, platform consolidation and AI-led demand.

The analyst highlighted that talks with partners were encouraging, with pipeline generation and momentum in large deals remaining healthy in the fiscal second quarter despite tough, year-over-year comparisons.

"We continue to view CrowdStrike as one of the primary beneficiaries of platform consolidation, though we expect investors to remain focused on competitive dynamics and whether current growth levels represent a new baseline or a period of demand acceleration," the analyst said.

Siddiqui ranks No. 226 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 80% of the time, delivering an average return of about 40%. See CrowdStrike Financials on TipRanks.

Shares of personal computer maker Dell Technologies (DELL) have rallied 251% this year, driven by strong demand for AI servers. Recently, Evercore analyst Amit Daryanani reiterated a buy rating on DELL and raised his price target to $550 from $500.

The 5-star analyst contends that while Dell's AI compute segment is getting investors' attention, the company's Storage business is underappreciated and well-positioned to drive revenue and profit acceleration. Daryanani expects the storage business to see higher demand once enterprises start to bring workload back on-premises.

The analyst highlighted that Dell's Storage business, estimated to account for only 10% of FY27 revenue, is one of the factors that could help improve profit margins, given its higher profitability compared to AI servers. Daryanani estimates that the Storage business's operating margin is currently in the low-20% range, while margins for the AI server and traditional server businesses are in the mid-single-digit and mid-teens range, respectively.

Overall, Daryanani views the Storage business as an important catalyst for three reasons: Dell has one of the broadest storage portfolios in the industry; Storage has a significantly higher margin profile than AI servers; and the shift toward proprietary, software-defined storage and AI-focused platforms.

"We think DELL's storage business is becoming increasingly strategic as AI infrastructure deployments move beyond GPU procurement and toward full stack + AI-ready architectures," said Daryanani.

Daryanani ranks No. 19 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 38.70%. See Dell Technologies Statistics on TipRanks.

SanDisk (SNDK) is benefiting from AI-driven demand for its memory and storage products. Following the company's 2026 Investor Day event, J.P. Morgan analyst Harlan Sur resumed research coverage of SNDK with a buy rating (it was earlier, "Not Rated") and a price target of $2,250.

The 5-star analyst believes SanDisk is well-positioned to capitalize on a structural inflection reached in NAND demand, driven by strong growth in AI inference. Sur's bullishness is based on three key factors.

First, Sur believes that SanDisk's New Business Model (NBM) framework (or long-term agreements) has enhanced its margin profile and reduced cyclicality. SNDK's eight signed NBMs represent a total contract value of $94 billion and a gross margin of about 80%, even at the floor pricing.

Second, Sur highlighted the growing NAND market and expects the total addressable market to surge from about $70 billion in calendar year 2025 to over $300 billion in 2026 and about $500 billion in 2027. Importantly, the analyst noted that data centers are the largest driver of NAND growth, as hyperscalers increasingly deploy flash for AI inference workloads.

Finally, Sur is optimistic on SanDisk due to its technology dominance. The company's BiCS10 technology is sampling ahead of schedule and has 65% more bits per wafer than BiCS8, the analyst said. Also, Sur expects SanDisk's High Bandwidth Flash to become a differentiated memory platform for AI inference.

"Layered on top, mgmt's commitment to returning 100% of excess cash flow to shareholders should drive an aggressive share count reduction trajectory that further amplifies EPS growth on a per-share basis," said Sur.

Sur ranks No. 15 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 43.20%. See SanDisk Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3024. Nov. 25 Could Be a Huge Day for the Stock Market

The Bureau of Labor Statistics released the latest inflation reading this morning, showing the Producer Price Index (PPI) jumped 6.5% in October from a year earlier. That's the biggest annual jump since 2022.

And it comes just three days after a hotter-than-expected Consumer Price Index (CPI) report. The market is now treating a rate hike at the Fed's Dec. 9 meeting as a done deal. The question is no longer whether the Fed hikes, but by how much.

Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.

The answer may well rest on the last inflation numbers the committee gets before it makes a decision, and that report lands on Nov. 25, the day before Thanksgiving.

The CPI report was released on Tuesday of this week, showing consumer prices rising 0.4% in October, above the 0.3% Wall Street was looking for. Core CPI -- which strips out food and energy -- also came in hot at 0.3%.

Then came today's wholesale numbers -- what businesses get paid before goods reach consumers. It was a mixed bag, with the overall monthly increase of 0.3% actually a touch below the expected 0.4%. But the core measure rose 0.3% and was in line with expectations. The overall annual rate climbed to 6.5%.

The Fed raised rates a quarter point in September, the first hike since 2023, and then held them steady in October's 10-2 vote. A week ago, the odds of a half point hike in December were 0%, according to FedWatch, now they are sitting above 12%. On the flip side, a week ago the market saw a more than 23% chance that rates wouldn't move at all. That's now 0%.

Before the CPI report, Fed Governor Christopher Waller said that a half point "should be considered" if core CPI ran around 0.3% or higher. It came in at exactly 0.3%. For context, the last time the Fed raised rates by a half point was in December 2022.

That's the setup for the final release of inflation numbers ahead of the Fed's December meeting. On Nov. 25, the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index report will be released, which just so happens to be the Fed's "preferred" gauge -- current Chair Kevin Warsh has explicitly said the Fed's target is 2% as measured by PCE.

If the PCE comes in hotter than expected, expect some turbulence.

Now, short-term movements in the market aren't really the focus for long-term investors. Day-to-day movements are more noise than anything. However, with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.40%) heavily concentrated in an AI trade that is more and more reliant on debt, the Fed's path matters more than it usually would.

Building data centers and filling them with chips is expensive, and companies are increasingly borrowing to pay for it -- even cash-rich big tech players like Meta Platforms (META +2.90%) and Alphabet (GOOG -0.96%) (GOOGL -0.98%). If the Fed ends up going further than expected, the math on some of these data center projects will get harder to justify.

#3021. Stock Market Today, Nov. 13: Cerebras Surges Over 11% on Record Q3 Revenue and Raised 2026 Guidance

Today, Nov. 13, 2026, Cerebras Systems rallied hard after a surprise profit, with Microsoft now among its customers.

Cerebras Systems (CBRS +11.22%), a rack-scale AI compute systems and cloud inference services provider, closed at $108.72, up 11.22%. Friday's gain followed record third-quarter revenue and raised 2026 guidance.

Trading volume reached 36.1 million shares, coming in about 275% above its three-month average of 9.6 million shares. Cerebras Systems IPO'd in 2026 and has fallen 65% since going public.

#3017. Carlisle: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Nondiscretionary re-roofing demand anchors a recurring revenue baseline that may insulate the business from construction cycles.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 28, 2026, Carlisle (CSL +0.90%) reported revenue grew 9% to $1.5 billion, driven by higher sales volume and pricing actions. Persistent reroofing demand and disciplined capital allocation provide clear visibility toward long-term earnings growth.

Carlisle faced higher petroleum-based raw material and freight costs during the third quarter. Management expects price realization to achieve cost parity by year end, creating a pricing tailwind for 2027.

Commercial reroofing represents over 70% of Carlisle Construction Materials sales, insulating the business from softness in new construction. Aging building infrastructure creates a recurring revenue baseline that owners can't easily postpone.

Carlisle continues to roll out labor-saving products that eliminate job-site installation steps for contractors. These proprietary offerings support higher pricing power and advance the company's goal to generate 25% of sales from recent innovations.

Disciplined execution and operational productivity are protecting cash flow through choppy market conditions, and executive leadership shows complete confidence in the operational foundation.

Structural advantages in direct distribution relationships, labor-saving innovation, and nondiscretionary reroofing demand support long-term targets, including $40 in adjusted earnings per share (EPS), a net income metric excluding one-time items, and return on invested capital (ROIC), a measure of profit generated per dollar of capital, above 25%.

#3015. The 'debasement trade' returns after Bessent bond maneuver. Crypto and gold are back in style

The debasement trade is gaining new traction on Wall Street as concern over the size and cost of the budget deficit mounts.

The conventional wisdom behind the trade is that perceived hard assets, like cryptocurrencies and precious metals, gain as investors try to hedge against a weaker U.S. dollar and Treasury debt in the face of ballooning government spending. Those fears reached a fever pitch last week before, and after, the Treasury Department's unusual step of increasing debt buybacks under Secretary Scott Bessent.

"The size of the Treasury purchases announced so far by Bessent are trivial in comparison to the size of the overall market, but the [signaling] effect was very powerful," said Stephen Coltman, head of macro at 21Shares, a crypto-focused creator of exchange-traded funds.

Gold touched three-month highs Monday, building on last week's advance of more than 5%. The yellow metal has climbed for five straight weeks and in August is on track for its biggest monthly rise since 1999.

Bitcoin added 2% Monday, to the highest since May. Last week, the digital currency soared 22%, its biggest three-day rally since 2023. Overnight Tuesday, the crypto touched $80,000.

The U.S. dollar index, which tracks the dollar against six other leading currencies, hit three-month lows last week and recorded its third down week in the last four. The index was little changed on Monday as investors expressed little appetite to bid up the greenback.

The Treasury Department said last week that it would double the maximum size of its bond buyback, to at least $4 billion from $2 billion. Two senior Treasury officials told CNBC Monday that the department could use its General Account to help fund the plans.

Last week's announcement followed news that the monthly U.S. budget deficit in July reached a five-year high and came at the same time as total federal government debt topped $40 trillion. Bessent told CNBC last week that he wields a "big toolkit" to calm the government bond market amid concerns about the government's financial health.

Long-dated U.S. Treasury yields surged last week, at one point sending the 30-year yield to almost a 20-year high of 5.34%, up from 4.82% in late June. Yields dipped and then rebounded in the wake of the Treasury buyback move, a signal that bond investors believed Bessent's moves were inadequate.

"Markets are saying something," the billionaire philanthropist and former energy trader John Arnold said in a Friday post on X. The weaker dollar, lower Treasury prices and strengthening hard assets, are "all part of the debasement trade," he wrote.

The Treasury's moves could aid the bond market, but also translate into outsized pain for the dollar, according to Nohshad Shah, Citadel Securities' head of fixed income sales for Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

The stakes are high for monetary policymakers, as a weaker greenback can ease financial conditions, Shah said. U.S. inflation — above the Federal Reserve's 2% target for five years — could worsen.

The Fed might need to hike interest rates in response, Shah said. Fed funds futures reflect about a 56% chance the central bank raises borrowing costs at its October meeting, up more than seven percentage points from a week ago, according to CME's FedWatch tool.

"The bond market's message is straightforward: fiscal or monetary policy should be tighter," Shah said in a Monday note. "Households may ultimately pay for policymakers' unwillingness to fix the roof whilst the sun is shining."

Coltman at 21Shares said the appetite for alternative stores of value also stems from mounting geopolitical tension. The U.S. on Monday rolled out a global sanctions program focused on segregating Iran from the global economy, days after slapping 50% tariffs on billions of dollars in Canadian exports.

Still, assets that would benefit from a debasement trade have recently received votes of confidence on Wall Street.

Deutsche Bank analyst Michael Hsueh said in a Monday note that gold could surpass his target price of $4,800 an ounce. Already, that level suggests the precious metal only need to rise another 3% or so from where it closed on Friday. "We see the Treasury policy change as underlining the gold constructive view," Hsueh said.

Billionaire investor Ray Dalio recommended investors remain overweight gold and bitcoin ahead of what he warned could become a debt crisis in the U.S. as a result of unchecked government spending. Gold could account for as much as 15% of a model portfolio, the Bridgewater Associates founder said.

"The government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote on LinkedIn Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Others are less sure, devaluing the notion of a debasement trade.

It's probably too early to endorse the idea unless it's clear that the Fed will go along with the Treasury Department, said Alexander Lis, investing chief at Social Discovery Ventures.

#3014. Amgen vs. Vertex Pharmaceuticals: Which Healthcare Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Amgen trades at a lower forward P/E than Vertex, but Vertex's near-zero debt and cystic fibrosis dominance make the trade-off harder to call.

Investors looking for stability in the healthcare sector often compare Amgen (AMGN -0.30%) and Vertex Pharmaceuticals (VRTX -0.74%). Choosing between these two industry leaders requires balancing diversified income against high-growth specialization.

Amgen offers a broad range of products across oncology and rare diseases, providing a steady business model. Vertex Pharmaceuticals focuses on transformative treatments for specific genetic conditions, yielding high profitability. Both companies represent different ways to play the ongoing innovation in the medical field.

Amgen discovers and manufactures medicines for a wide array of serious diseases, ranging from inflammation to oncology. It sells primarily to three large distributors, McKesson Corp. (MCK +2.39%), Cencora Inc. (COR +2.96%), and Cardinal Health (CAH +1.06%), which accounted for nearly 77% of its revenues in 2025. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, though it also simplifies the logistics of its global distribution network.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $38.9 billion, representing growth of roughly 8.3% over the prior period. The company generated net income of nearly $9.4 billion during this same time frame. This growth reflects the continued expansion of its core portfolio and its strategic push into new markets like China and Japan.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was roughly 6.3x. This ratio measures leverage by comparing total debt to shareholder equity, showing that Amgen uses significant borrowed capital to fund its operations. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term obligations, was close to 1.1x, while free cash flow for the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, reached roughly $8.1 billion.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals is a leader among biotech stocks, known for its dominance in cystic fibrosis treatments. It has successfully expanded its focus to include sickle cell disease through its joint development of Casgevy with CRISPR Therapeutics (CRSP -0.89%). The company also utilizes out-licensing arrangements for other candidates with partners like Zai Lab (ZLAB -5.53%) to maximize the reach of its intellectual property.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached about $13.0 billion, which indicates growth of approximately 10.8% compared to the previous year. Net income for this period was around $4.0 billion. These figures highlight the strong demand for its specialized therapies and its ability to maintain high margins in niche medical markets.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the company maintained a very conservative debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 0.1x. This low level of debt relative to shareholder equity provides Vertex Pharmaceuticals with significant financial flexibility. Its current ratio was approximately 2.9x, and it generated free cash flow of nearly $3.2 billion during the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025.

Amgen faces substantial competition from biosimilars and generics, which can erode the market share of its older products. It also deals with intense pricing pressure from payers and pharmacy benefit managers, alongside regulatory risks from the Inflation Reduction Act. Operational risks include a heavy reliance on specific manufacturing facilities in Puerto Rico and California, as well as ongoing tax disputes with the IRS.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals carries significant risk due to its revenue concentration in cystic fibrosis medicines, making it vulnerable to any new competitive entry in that specific field. Commercializing complex new therapies like Casgevy involves high manufacturing costs and operational complexity. Additionally, the company faces uncertainty regarding the clinical development of its pipeline candidates and the ever-present pressure from government health programs to lower prices.

Amgen appears to be the more affordable option for value-conscious investors based on its significantly lower Forward P/E and P/S ratio.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals continues to benefit from its focus on cystic fibrosis (CF). It recently launched Alyftrek, a once-daily, next-in-class, triple-combination CF regimen. The treatment is seen encouragingly by investors. Alyftrek marks a notable improvement to the twice-daily dosage of Vertex's other CF products and was approved in Australia, New Zealand, Switzerland, the European Union, the U.K., and Canada over the course of 2025, after the U.S. approved it in 2024. Plenty more jurisdictions are evaluating the treatment today. In the U.S., label extensions (the expansion of FDA approval for use) mean Vertex can treat 95% of people with cystic fibrosis in the country today.

Vertex has expanded into treating other conditions. Povetacicept, an investigational recombinant fusion protein therapeutic, continues to show best-in-class potential as a treatment for three chronic autoimmune diseases: IgA nephropathy (IgAN), primary membranous nephropathy (pMN), and generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG). The FDA has granted its Breakthrough Therapy Designation for IgAN, with potential approval by Nov. 30 this year, as well as Fast Track Designation for pMN. If approved across the IgAN, pMN, and gMG indications, peak annual sales could reach $4 billion to $7 billion, according to equity analyst firm Argus.

For fiscal 2026, Vertex sales should rise about 10% to $13.3 billion, with net income coming in roughly even with 2025.

Amgen is not a fast-growing company by sales, but, given its scale, it should be able to achieve a roughly 3% revenue increase in 2026, to around $37.8 billion. Such size forgives a lot with slower-growing pharmaceutical businesses. For one, its billions in net income could enable Amgen to pursue future growth by acquiring other drugmakers. The business also has six drug franchises that generate $1 billion-plus in annual revenue. There is real value there.

Late last year, Amgen became one of nine companies to reach a deal with the U.S. government to lower drug prices (Bristol Myers Squibb (BMY +1.78%), Merck & Co. (MRK +0.55%), and Gilead Sciences Inc. (GILD +0.08%) are also among the companies that signed a deal). Based on this agreement, Amgen will be exempt from Trump's pharmaceutical tariffs for three years, a positive, in exchange for the company reducing prices on its key migraine drug Aimovig and rheumatoid arthritis medicine Amjevita by about 60% and 80%, respectively, in a direct-to-consumer platform launched in October.

Vertex is growing faster, revenue-wise, and while it comes at a premium compared to Amgen, its CF franchise and product pipeline, including Povetacicept, make it the most promising stock for long-term investors.

#3008. Suncrete (RMIX) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Acquisitions lift revenue sharply, but weaker residential demand forces a guidance cut.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Suncrete, Inc. (RMIX -37.44%) reported a sequential revenue increase of 45% and expanded its supplemental adjusted EBITDA margin by 150 basis points to 16.5% during the third quarter. Management stated that acquisition integration is progressing according to its 12 to 18-month playbook, with North Texas showing operational improvement despite competitive pressures. The company updated its full-year guidance to account for delays in large commercial projects and continued weakness in the residential sector caused by high interest rates. Management indicated that the M&A pipeline remains active, though several anticipated closures have shifted into the first half of 2027.

Operator: Greetings, and welcome to the Suncrete Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please note, this conference is being recorded. I will now turn the conference over to Rick Black, Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

Rick Black: Thank you, operator, and good morning, everyone. We appreciate you joining us for the Suncrete conference call to review third quarter 2026 results. This call is also being webcast, and it can be accessed through the audio link on the Events and Presentations page of the Investor Relations section of suncrete.com. Information recorded on this call speaks only as of today, November 13, 2026. Please be advised that any time-sensitive information may no longer be accurate as of the date of any replay listening or transcript reading.

I would also like to remind you that the statements made in today's discussion that are not historical facts, including statements of expectations or future events or future financial performance are considered forward-looking statements made pursuant to the safe harbor provision of the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995. We will be making forward-looking statements as part of today's call that, by their nature, are uncertain and outside of the company's control. Actual results may differ materially. Please refer to our earnings press release for our disclosures on forward-looking statements. These factors and other risks and uncertainties are described in detail in the company's filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Management will also refer to non-GAAP measures, including adjusted net income, adjusted EBITDA and supplemental adjusted EBITDA. Reconciliations to the nearest GAAP measures can be found at the end of our earnings press release. Suncrete assumes no obligation to publicly update or revise any forward-looking statements. And now with that, I would now like to turn the call over to Suncrete's CEO, Randall Edgar. Randall?

Randall Edgar: Thank you, Rick, and good morning, everyone. Joining me today are Tommy Wentroth, our Chief Financial Officer; and Ned Fleming, our Executive Chairman, along with other members of our management team. I'll begin with our third quarter operating performance and the progress we are making across our acquired businesses. Tommy will then review our financial results and 2026 outlook, followed by Ned's remarks before we open the call for questions. There are 3 things I want investors to take away from the quarter. First, our local markets across the Sunbelt remain active, and I'm pleased with our operational performance that generated approximately 10% organic growth.

In addition, we increased the supplemental adjusted EBITDA margin by 150 points -- basis points compared to the second quarter. Also, revenue was up 45% and supplemental adjusted EBITDA was up 60% compared to last quarter. Second important takeaway is that our acquisition integration plans are progressing as expected with several businesses now approaching Suncrete target margin levels. We took a significant step forward on this front with our North Texas work. Third, our M&A pipeline remains robust, while our balance sheet gives us the flexibility to continue executing our growth strategy. Before going any further, I want to thank our more than 1,100 employees for their commitment to our customers and to one another. Ready-mix is a service business.

The quality of our product and the reliability of deliveries depend on the local teams who manage the plants, maintain the equipment and work with customers every day. We believe people and culture are fundamental to our ability to consistently execute to grow the business and to create long-term value. Activity across the Sunbelt market supported both year-over-year and sequential volume growth. However, we lowered our full year outlook ranges to reflect the current weakness in our residential construction due to elevated interest rates, and multiple larger projects have been delayed as a result of the uncertainty following the midterm election and continuing geopolitical concerns. We remain confident in the persistent volume demand across our specific markets.

Since we have navigated through similar economic times, we know that many new growth and margin opportunities will arise. The revised outlook reflects adjusted year-end time lines for integration in certain markets. We continue to advance operating initiatives and remain confident in our ability to bring this year's acquisitions for the target margin profile. Throughout Suncrete's footprint, our markets remain underbuilt compared to the demand created by population growth in Oklahoma, Arkansas, North Texas and Louisiana. This trend is not slowing. Population and business growth across the Sunbelt continues to support long-term demand opportunities in commercial, infrastructure and residential construction. Data centers are a growing part of that mix.

In our Oklahoma market, several projects are under construction today, and we expect them to support activity into 2027. Additionally, we continue to see at least 2 years of data center backlog scheduled across our footprint. Our local relationships and ability to reliably execute large, complex pours is a competitive advantage and positions us well for these projects. Our strategy has always been to build a balanced customer base across commercial, infrastructure and residential construction, which has served us well in times of economic uncertainty. Activity varies by sector and geography, and a key component for our continued success is strong customer relationships. Turning now to our growing platform and the integration of acquisitions.

We continue to implement the playbook and are making progress with all of the businesses we acquired last quarter. Notably, North Texas remains highly competitive, but our performance improved steadily throughout the quarter, as shown by the margin expansion. Our playbook is built around 5 key areas: people and culture, procurement, commercial opportunities, logistics and technology. It starts with people, where we work to retain local leadership and bring each team into a shared culture of safety and accountability. In procurement, greater purchasing scale can improve how we source materials, equipment and maintenance service. On the commercial side, our broader capabilities can help local teams serve regional customers and pursue projects that may previously have been beyond their reach.

In logistics, we concentrate on dispatch, routing, maintenance and fleet productivity. Technology supports those efforts by giving teams better visibility into truck activity and efficiency, plant performance and financial results. We evaluate progress through measures such as yards delivered per driver hour, plant and fleet utilization, on-time delivery and profitability. Turning now to strategy. The 2026 acquisitions provide a larger revenue base for 2027 and further margin expansion opportunities. At present, we expect these acquisitions to contribute approximately $200 million to $250 million of revenue in 2027. Depending on project timing and market activity, representing a meaningful full year contribution.

Continued integration should support further margin improvement, and we expect gross profit to expand and SG&A to grow more slowly than revenue. Turning to the cost environment. We generally address material price increases through annual customer base price adjustments. We expect limited aggregate cost impact in Q4 and plan to incorporate anticipated supplier increases into our normal 2027 pricing cycle. Our acquisition pipeline remains active. We are in advanced discussions with several prospects that would be highly accretive, expanding existing markets and entering adjacent markets. We continue to exercise discipline in capital deployment and believe our balance sheet supports our future growth.

While the timing of several acquisitions has shifted to the first half of 2027, our number of opportunities continues to expand. We are committed to growing the platform and integrating our playbook to scale the business and drive operating performance. As we build density in our local markets, we have an opportunity to serve more customers, improve the productivity of our plants and trucks and expand margins across the businesses we acquire. I'll now turn the call over to Tommy.

Thomas Wentroth: Thank you, Randall, and good morning, everyone. As a reminder, our third quarter results reflect the full contributions from all acquisitions closed to date. Now reviewing our third quarter results and key financial metrics. Revenue in the third quarter was $141 million, an increase of 45% compared to the second quarter of 2026 and up 163% compared to Q3 last year. Net income in the third quarter was $76,000 (sic) [ $78,000 ] compared to a net loss of $37.1 million in the second quarter of 2026 and net income in the same quarter last year of $4.6 million. Adjusted EBITDA in the third quarter was $22.4 million, representing a 15.9% adjusted EBITDA margin.

This increased 66% when compared to the second quarter of 2026 and 64% compared to Q3 last year. Supplemental adjusted EBITDA in the third quarter was $23.3 million, representing a 16.5% supplemental adjusted EBITDA margin. This increased 60% compared to the second quarter of 2026 and 62% compared to Q3 last year. You can find GAAP to non-GAAP reconciliations of net income, adjusted EBITDA and supplemental adjusted EBITDA financial measures at the end of today's earnings release. Turning now to the balance sheet. Turning now to our outlook.
