#3005. Stock Market Today, Nov. 13: CoreWeave Falls Over 6% on AI-Cloud Selling and Reported $4 Billion Loan Talks

Today, Nov. 13, 2026, CoreWeave's shares slid as investors weighed a possible equipment-backed loan against its rapid buildout.

CoreWeave (CRWV -6.13%), a specialized cloud computing provider for generative AI workloads, also known as a neocloud, closed at $53.13, down 6.13%. Premarket AI-cloud selling and debt-funding concerns outweighed the company's strong revenue growth, and investors are watching guidance and financing needs next.

Trading volume reached 77.6 million shares, coming in about 72% above its three-month average of 45.1 million shares. CoreWeave IPO'd in 2025 and has grown 33% since going public.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.40%) fell 0.40% to 7,562, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -0.91%) declined 0.91% to 25,136. In AI cloud infrastructure and data center services, Nebius Group closed at $115.88, down 9.92%, and IREN finished at $18.17, down 6.72%, reflecting pressure on AI-cloud peers after Nvidia-related turmoil and capital-spending concerns.

On Friday, Nov. 13, CoreWeave extended yesterday's declines in the wake of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation on Nov. 12. Nvidia was a major financial backer, shareholder, and strategic partner to the neocloud provider, and Huang's departure left uncertainty around how the semiconductor chip giant's relationship with CoreWeave could change.

Reports that CoreWeave was pursuing at least $4 billion in loans to expand its AI infrastructure also weighed on the stock. Against a macroeconomic backdrop of rising interest rates, the neocloud's decision to add to its debt burden raised fresh concerns on Wall Street, and justifiably so. The company exited the third quarter with over $40 billion in debt.

Earlier this week, CoreWeave's Q3 results showed revenue up a whopping 168% to $3.7 billion, and it raised 2026 revenue guidance to a range between $13.0 billion and $13.3 billion, more than double the $5 billion produced in 2025. That update impressed Wall Street initially, as it supported the artificial intelligence demand story.

However, the shares later reversed amid concerns that CoreWeave's debt burden was getting too high, keeping leverage and buildout risk central to the investor debate.

#3001. Why Intel Stock Slumped Today

It wasn't a good day for tech stocks generally.

It was an explosive week for the semiconductor industry, and Intel (INTC -2.52%) is surely glad that it's over. On Friday, the company's stock sank for the second day in a row, dipping by almost 3% on growing concerns that changes at Nvidia (NVDA -2.01%) would negatively affect its operations.

The scandal swirling around now-former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang continued to reverberate across the semiconductor space, not to mention the broader tech sector. For Intel, it continues to raise questions about its own future.

This stands to reason, as Nvidia is both a business partner and a major shareholder, owning roughly $13 billion in Intel stock.

The future of that holding is in some doubt with the replacement of Huang by interim chief Colette Kress. Another area of concern is the collaboration between the two companies on custom x86 central processing units (CPUs).

If there's anything investors don't like, it's uncertainty, and that's swirling around Nvidia and every company it touches. That's a long list, as it's considered by many to be the hardware maker at the forefront of the artificial intelligence (AI) revolution. Personally, I believe the industry will get over the shock of Huang's scandal and resignation, but I'd hold off on making any investment decisions on Intel until its future with Nvidia becomes clearer.

#2997. Why Newsmax Stock Popped on Friday

Politics equals profits for the cable news challenger.

Shares of Newsmax (NMAX +31.41%) soared 31% on Friday after the media platform highlighted its improved profitability.

Newsmax's third-quarter revenue surged 52.5% year over year to $69 million. Excluding a non-recurring advertising fee, revenue rose 21.6% to $55 million.

The news outlet's audience reached 22.5 million total viewers, including 9.1 million adults aged 35 to 64, making Newsmax the No. 2 cable news channel for audience engagement among that key cohort.

All told, Newsmax's net income improved to $15.2 million, or $0.12 per share. That's compared to a net loss of $4.1 million, or $0.03 per share, in the year-ago quarter.

"Our third-quarter results reflect the operating leverage we are building across the business, with affiliate and licensing growth continuing to shift our revenue mix toward our highest-margin streams," chief financial officer Darryle Burnham said.

Newsmax is working to broaden its reach with new services tailored to the military and people interested in events in Ukraine. The company also increased its social media followers by 13% to 26.8 million as of Sept. 30.

Yet the elections and political news commentary remain the keys to Newsmax's growth strategy.

"Engagement rose through the midterm campaign, and that is an early indicator of what is ahead," CEO Christopher Ruddy said. "We have positioned Newsmax on all platforms for all people, from cable and free streaming to Newsmax+, social, and now AI, heading into what we expect to be the largest presidential cycle on record."

#2995. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#2992. Advance Auto Parts vs. Airbnb: Is a Retail Rebound or Travel Giant Stock the Better Buy for 2027?

One runs thousands of stores with a sizable debt load, the other rents out millions of listings without owning a single room. Their valuation gap is wide, and the risks differ sharply.

Whether you are betting on people fixing older cars or traveling to new destinations, Advance Auto Parts (AAP -0.37%) and Airbnb (ABNB +3.12%) offer very different ways to play the consumer economy.

Advance Auto Parts provides essential components for vehicle maintenance, catering to both professional mechanics and hobbyists. Airbnb operates a digital platform that has changed how travelers book lodging worldwide. While one relies on physical retail and the other on a high-growth tech marketplace, both companies face unique challenges heading into 2027.

Advance Auto Parts operates a vast network of stores across North America, serving both professional mechanics and do-it-yourself customers. The company focuses on the automotive aftermarket, providing everything from engine components to accessories among retail stocks in the automotive niche. It serves over 4,300 company-operated stores and supports hundreds of independently owned locations through its distribution network, with its professional segment accounting for approximately 50% of total sales in 2025.

In the 12 months ended Oct. 10, 2026, revenue reached around $8.6 billion. This was a slight decline of approximately 0.3% compared to the previous 12-month period, which management attributed to a challenging macroeconomic environment for consumers. During this time, the company reported a net income of about $100 million.

As of its January 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 2.4x. This ratio shows that for every dollar of equity, the company has $2.40 in total debt, which indicates that it relies more on borrowing than on shareholder investments. The current ratio, which compares short-term assets to liabilities, is close to 1.7x. In the fiscal year ended Jan. 3, 2026, free cash flow was negative $298.0 million, reflecting a period where cash outflows for operations and equipment exceeded cash inflows.

Airbnb operates a global two-sided marketplace connecting hosts who offer unique stays and experiences with travelers around the world. The company utilizes a co-host network to help hosts manage their listings more effectively and ensure a high quality of service for guests. It currently manages a massive platform that spans more than 220 countries and regions, allowing it to offer over 9 million active listings without owning real estate directly.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $14.0 billion. This reflects growth of around 16.9% over the prior year. Net income during this same period was close to $3.2 billion.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 0.3x, and the current ratio is approximately 1.4x, suggesting the company maintains a healthy liquidity position. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was nearly $4.6 billion. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 34.3% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since stock-based compensation is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Advance Auto Parts faces significant competition from national chains like AutoZone (AZO +0.23%) and O'Reilly Automotive (ORLY +0.69%), as well as mass merchandisers and online retailers. It also deals with uncertainty regarding its 2024 restructuring plan and the ability to maintain long-term operational efficiencies. Geopolitical risks, inflation, and the shift toward electric vehicles could also disrupt its complex supply chain and traditional business model.

Airbnb is highly dependent on the global travel and hospitality industries, which are sensitive to economic downturns, geopolitical crises, and health emergencies. The company faces ongoing regulatory challenges and short-term rental bans in various cities worldwide that could limit its listing inventory. Additionally, its reliance on third-party providers like Amazon.com Inc. (AMZN -0.29%) for cloud infrastructure adds operational risk, alongside potential cybersecurity threats.

Advance Auto Parts appears cheaper based on its P/S ratio, while Airbnb has a higher Forward P/E relative to its future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

Airbnb reported an excellent third quarter of fiscal 2026. The business beat consensus revenue estimates by more than $100 million, reporting $4.87 billion in sales, up nearly 19% from the same period in 2025, driven by a strong increase in booked nights. Foreign markets like India and Latin America, two markets in which Airbnb is seeking growth, have been strong this year. The seasonally weaker fourth quarter should see sales close to $3.3 billion and net income around $488 million.

The business is finding ways to expand its popular travel business, embracing features like buy now, pay later (BNPL) on reservations, a form of short-term lending. The network advantage of Airbnb's history, over 2 billion guest arrivals since 2008, and the lack of host presence on AI (meaning a competitor using AI has little data to scrape to form a competing product) give it a competitive moat.

For the full year, management expects sales of $14.1 billion with net income of $3.2 billion.

Advance Auto Parts is in the midst of turning around the struggles it has experienced over the past few years, including sales growth, margins, and market share. The aftermarket auto parts business has been in a slump, largely due to a mix of factors: weak do-it-yourself demand, vehicle electrification, intense competition, and general pocketbook pressures on consumers.

Company management has been addressing the business's issues by rolling out a new multi-year turnaround program. That started in late 2024, including closing stores, optimizing store footprints, and overhauling its supply chain--for instance, reducing the number of carriers by 70% through consolidated shipments.

To date, the turnaround program has had mixed success. The first quarter of fiscal 2026 brought results much better than expected: revenue of $2.6 billion and net income of $25 million. The second quarter and third quarter disappointed. In the third quarter, reported in early November, the company fell short of expectations on revenue, with just over $2 billion in sales and net income of $15 million. Revenue was slightly below the year-ago quarter, while the business did swing from a net loss in the same comparable period.

Analysts see full-year 2026 sales slightly lower year-over-year at around $8.5 billion, with net income improving to around $132 million, more than three times 2025.

This is a choice between a brick-and-mortar retailer working to turn its slump around and an asset-light web broker of vacation rooms. Advance's forward P/E feel a little rich for a company still trying to find its legs again, while Airbnb's premium isn't so high that long-term investors would have trouble justifying a purchase. 2027 is probably a 'prove it' year for Advance Auto Parts. Go with Airbnb.

#2989. Sandisk Stock Fell Back Under $900 on Friday. I'd Start With a Small Position.

The flash memory company's outlook hasn't shifted since Oct. 29. Its stock price has.

Last week, I picked a price where I'd buy Sandisk (SNDK -5.59%) stock: around $900 a share, or four years of profits at the pace management guided for this quarter. By Wednesday, Nov. 11, the flash memory maker had closed near $978, so I waited.

But two trading days later, it's back under that level. Shares dropped about 8% on Friday, to roughly $860 as of this writing, and they're down about 12% since Wednesday's close.

These prices are all before Sandisk's eight-for-one stock split. Split-adjusted trading should begin on Nov. 20 -- at that point, today's price would come to about $107 and my buy price to about $112.

By my own rule, then, the stock is a buy again. I'd just do it carefully.

Sandisk hasn't changed its guidance since it posted fiscal first-quarter results on Oct. 29, and its only regulatory filings since then have been its quarterly report, an insider's stock-transaction report, and an ownership filing by a big shareholder.

Friday's fall also wasn't limited to Sandisk. As of this writing, memory chipmaker Micron is down around 4%, hard drive maker Western Digital is off about 7%, and the Nasdaq Composite has dipped about 1%.

So, the drop looks like part of a wider sell-off in tech stocks, not a reaction to anything Sandisk said or reported. The earnings behind my $900 price are the same as they were on Wednesday.

For the fiscal second quarter of 2027, management sees revenue between $12.2 billion and $12.6 billion and non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings between $55 and $57 a pre-split share. That quarter started after fiscal Q1 ended on Oct. 2, so Sandisk is already about six weeks into it. At the midpoint, revenue would reach about $12.4 billion, up roughly 12% over fiscal Q1's $11.0 billion. That would be about four times the $3.0 billion Sandisk took in during the same quarter a year before. And adjusted profit per share would be about nine times the year-ago number of $6.20.

Showing where this growth comes from, data center revenue hit $4.8 billion in fiscal Q1, making up about 44% of Sandisk's sales. A year before, it was below $300 million. Total revenue climbed 378% year over year in fiscal Q1.

My $900 price is four years of earnings at the $56 midpoint. At roughly $860, a buyer pays for about 3.8 years of them.

And even if the quarter lands at the bottom of the range, $55 a share, four years of that pace would be about $880 -- still above where the stock trades now.

The valuation has also come down quickly. Shares closed near $1,151 on Oct. 30, the first trading day after the guidance came out, or about 5 times annualized guided earnings. Since then, the stock has fallen about 26% on the same outlook.

The biggest risk is flash pricing. Sandisk's profits have soared largely because it's charging much more for its memory.

And in its latest quarterly report, management said it expects demand driven by artificial intelligence (AI) to last through 2027 and beyond. But flash prices can drop fast when supply catches up to demand.

Showing how much depends on these prices, Sandisk's adjusted gross margin was around 30% in the first quarter of fiscal 2026. It hit 51% the next quarter and 85% in the latest one. For this quarter, the company guides for 84.5% to 85.5%. In short, the margin looks to have stopped widening, while sequential revenue growth is guided to slow to around 12% from 23%.

Sure, the guidance doesn't show prices dropping yet. But my price assumes four years of earnings around this quarter's pace, and a memory boom might not last that long. If flash prices slip, the profits behind my $900 price could slip with them, and the stock could fall further before it recovers.

Is Sandisk a buy now that it's back under $900? I think it is. I'd consider starting with a small position today and adding to it over time. If the stock keeps sliding, a small first purchase leaves room to buy more at lower prices.

#2987. Advisor Insights — Brought to You By The Motley Fool

Turn this week’s uncertainty into your next useful client conversation.

Welcome back to Advisor Insights, brought to you by The Motley Fool! This week served up a rally, a rate scare, and a CEO exit: stocks jumped Monday as oil fell, slipped Tuesday after hot October inflation, bounced back Wednesday, then stumbled Thursday when Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned and the 10-year Treasury yield closed back above 5%, with a December rate hike looking more likely by the day.

Inside: what hot inflation and strong jobs mean for the Fed, Nvidia after Jensen Huang's exit, short-Treasury ETFs for a rising-rate world, upcoming mutual fund distributions, the IRS's 2027 tax inflation adjustments, and a tax surprise for prediction-market bettors.

The Fed has been handed a one-two punch: a jobs report that beat every forecast, then an inflation report that beat the consensus. Clients will ask whether higher rates are a blip or a regime. Here is the short version for the call.

The honest caveat: Energy is the swing factor. Hornbach expects October and the next two CPI readings to be among the year's more favorable ones, while Baird's Jason Trennert warns that refined-product prices could stay sticky even if crude falls. Translation: Nobody knows, so build for both.

With the 10-year Treasury back above 5% and a December hike widely expected, the front end of the curve is where clients can earn income without betting on the next rate move. Four funds cover most of the territory:

Sources: etf.com and Snowball Analytics; issuer fact sheets

It's capital gains distribution season, and the early estimates are in. After a strong year (broad U.S. benchmarks are up about 10%), outflows to ETFs are forcing many active mutual funds to sell winners and pass the gains to remaining shareholders, mostly between late November and year-end. Morningstar's early look found a handful of strategies distributing more than 30% of NAV, led by a 57% estimate. Share class matters, too: one Columbia fund shows roughly 42% for institutional shares and 56% for A shares. Estimates are preliminary and can still change.

This is a client communication opportunity hiding in plain sight, because distributions in taxable accounts are taxable even when reinvested. Clients in 401(k)s and IRAs can relax, a handy reminder of why asset location matters. Screen taxable holdings against the estimates and call clients with meaningful exposure to talk through harvesting losses to offset gains, waiting to buy until after the ex-date, and whether a lower-distribution ETF is worth the tax cost of switching. Try: "Before year-end, I checked which of your funds are expected to pay taxable gains. Here is what I found, and here is what we can do about it."

Resources for advisors: Morningstar: Which Funds Will Pay the Biggest 2026 Capital Gains Distributions? | Vanguard: preliminary 2026 year-end distribution estimates (PDF)

Source: Morningstar and exchange close data through Nov. 5th. 2026. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned Thursday morning after a week of reports about his personal conduct and questions about whether Nvidia chips reached China through a customer in Singapore. The first report involved an affair with an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer, whom U.S. officials suspected of spying for China (China denies it). A second relationship, with one of Huang's direct reports, surfaced Wednesday, and the board met that night. CFO Colette Kress is now interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent replacement, and shares closed down about 4% after plunging as much as 14% early in the session.

Investors should take Huang's departure seriously, but we think the sell-off has gotten ahead of the business, pricing in more damage than the numbers show so far. Analyst Dan Ives estimates Nvidia is worth 25% to 30% less without him, and the market has already applied much of that. Demand is still strong (last quarter's revenue rose 106% to $96.2 billion), and we estimate that even if regulators cut off sales routed through Singapore, Nvidia would lose no more than about 5% of revenue. Export policy and customers' pricing leverage are the bigger risks. What should be done with your shares depends on position size and why you own the company: If it's a large position bought for Huang's judgment, reassess how much risk you want to carry; if it's a smaller position held for Nvidia's products and AI demand, that thesis is intact.

Between a sharp tech selloff, a hot inflation report, and Nvidia's CEO stepping down, clients have plenty to wonder about, which makes this a good week to reach out before they call you. Here is an email or call opener:

“Markets have been choppy this month, with a tech selloff, a hot inflation report, and some big headlines. Nothing about your plan has to change, but I'd like to take 15 minutes to check in on three things: how you're feeling about the swings, whether anything in your income, health, or cash needs has shifted, and a few year-end tax moves worth making before Dec. 31. Do you have time this week or next?"

Prioritize calls to clients nearing retirement, clients with concentrated stock positions, and clients holding mutual funds expected to make big year-end distributions.

On Nov. 5th, the IRS released Revenue Procedure 2026-35, which adjusts almost 70 tax provisions for 2027 (generally for returns filed in 2028). The standard deduction rises about 3%, to $33,200 for joint filers, $16,600 for single filers, and $24,925 for heads of household. The top 37% bracket still starts at $661,375 for single filers and $793,650 for joint filers, the child tax credit rises $100 to $2,300 (up to $1,800 refundable), and the health FSA limit rises $100 to $3,500.

For most clients, the standard deduction and bracket changes matter most, since they set the baseline for next year’s tax bill. On the estate and gift side, the basic estate tax exclusion rises to $15,480,000 per person (up from $15 million), and the annual gift exclusion rises to $20,000 per recipient (up from $19,000) The release does not cover 2027 retirement-plan contribution limits, which have historically been announced separately.

Resources: IRS: 2027 inflation adjustments | KPMG: Rev. Proc. 2026-35 summary

Kalshi reports more than $2.5 billion in trading volume tied to the midterm elections, so some clients may have had skin in the game. The tax side is murkier than the betting side: how prediction-market contracts are classified (gambling income, capital gains, or something else) remains unsettled. If winnings are treated as gambling income, a 2026 law change limits loss deductions to 90% of losses (and only for itemizers, against winnings), so a client who breaks even can still owe tax. Under capital treatment, losses can offset capital gains instead.

The 2027 Social Security cost-of-living adjustment

Announced Oct. 14 and the largest since 2023.

Why It Matters to Advisors: At 5.51%, I bonds top today’s short-Treasury ETF yields (SGOV’s 30-day SEC yield is 3.83%), which makes them worth a look for clients. There are caveats worth sharing: the 5.51% applies only for the first six months, after which the rate follows inflation (the fixed portion stays and the rest can fall), and the lockup and $10,000 cap limit how much of a reserve they can cover. Interest is also federally taxable (deferred until redemption) but exempt from state and local income tax, and I bonds can’t be held in an IRA. With September CPI at 3.7% and a December hike looming, it’s a good week to ask: “Let’s look at whether I bonds belong in your cash reserve. Do you want to run the numbers?”

“Far more money has been lost by investors preparing for corrections, or trying to anticipate corrections, than has been lost in the corrections themselves.”

#2983. Stats Rundown: 3 numbers to know from the Mavericks’ 133-94 embarrassment at Minnesota

The Dallas Mavericks (7-5) had no stomach for the fight on Thursday, dropping an embarrassing 133-94 loss to the Minnesota Timberwolves (9-3) at Target Center. Bones Hyland led all scorers with 24 points off the Minnesota bench, while LaMelo Ball dropped 22 for the Wolves in just 25 minutes in the win.

Zaccharie Risacher led the Mavericks with 22 points off the bench in the loss.

Let's not belabor the point. This was a straightforward ass-whipping. Here are some stats from the Mavericks' no good, very bad day at the office.

If you blinked late in the first quarter, you may have missed the moment the Mavericks went from firmly in control to flailing. PJ Washington drove for a contested layup, and Naji Marshall followed that, the next time down, with a pair of free throws to extend the Mavs' early lead to 24-19 as Flagg took his normal rest stretch on the bench.

From there, Bones Hyland and Ayo Dosunmu keyed a 15-2 run over the last 2:48 of the first. Dosunmu hit his first bucket of the game, a 3-pointer from the right wing with 1:38 left in the opening frame, to swing the Wolves back in front, 26-24. Then, after Marshall stopped the bleeding with another 2-for-2 trip to the line, Jaylen Clark banged in a last-second 3-ball of his own to extend Minnesota's lead to 34-26.

The Mavs did not connect on a field goal in the final 3:34 of the quarter after Flagg went to the bench a minute before that. Dallas finished the first 9-0f-25 (36%) from the floor, while the Timberwolves shot a much healthier 12-of-22 (54.5%) to open the game.

The Mavericks scored 22 of their first 42 points off Minnesota turnovers on Thursday. That's a full 52.8% of the points the Mavs scored over the first 19 minutes of the game that came off the Timberwolves' nine turnovers.

Dallas got out and ran early in the second to battle back from what eventually bloomed into a 10-point hole. Flagg got into a passing lane for his first steal of the contest with seven minutes left in the half, which led to a ferocious dunk from Zaccharie Risacher that cut the Timberwolves' lead to 43-42.

But Minnesota embarked on another 12-3 run from that point, and guess what they didn't do during that run? If you guessed "turn the ball over," you would be correct. The Mavs weren't able to manufacture a whole lot of offense in the halfcourt throughout the first half. Dallas' defense was the only thing fueling the team's offense. Meanwhile, on the other end, The Wolves were pouring it on.

Minny shot 27-of-44 (61.4%) from the field in the first half, while the Mavs shot just 20-of-48 (41.7%). That disparity led to the Timberwolves' swolen 67-52 lead at the break.

A vibrant, colorful and above all, ridiculous rapid-fire discussion erupted in the MavsMoneyball Slack channel in the third quarter on Thursday, partially debating whether it was the Mavericks' offense that failed the defense or the defense that called it a night early and left the offense on an island. Whichever way you lean, neither of these things can happen when you're facing a bona fide early-season juggernaut like the Anthony Edwards-less Timberwolves. The Wolves came into the game with the NBA's best defensive rating (105.1).

Flagg did nothing on the defensive end. Marshall one-upped him by beating his head against a wall on both ends of the floor. Someone tried to make the argument that Martin was the best player on the floor for Dallas for long stretches of the first half. Risacher's stat line ended up looking good in the loss, but that was influenced loudly by the fact that garbage time against the Wolves stretched into most of the second half. For the record, Risacher led the Mavs with 22 points on 9-of-14 shooting (4-of-8 from 3-point range), to go along with six rebounds, two steals and a blocked shot in the loss.

After coming into the second half leading by 15, the Timberwolves outscored the Mavs 36-17 in the third to put Dallas to bed early. Just a piss poor effort on all fronts against a foe that will embarrass you if effort and execution elude you for 48 minutes. Which it did. So they did.

#2975. Game Preview: Knicks at Pacers, November 13, 2026

There are sports movies, there are even some good sports movies. And then there is Hoosiers.

As a basketball romantic you are definitely not flipping the channel when old ball coach Norman Dale a.k.a. Gene Hackman drives his 1951 Chevy Styleline Deluxe through the backwoods of Indiana all the way to the little town of Hickory, where he hopes to find a new life, new love and redemption. You let yourself be mesmerized by Angelo Pizzo's and David Anspaugh's timeless fairytale, by the very essence of "Hoosier Hysteria," the beautiful yet tragic tribulations of high school sports, the rough, simple charm of small town America. And you enjoy every second of it, despite a rather predictable underdog story unfolding in front of your eyes that Hollywood has repeated many times over.

The current Indiana Pacers would kill for some of that predictability, the kind they expected going into this season. After a surprising Finals berth in 2025, their following year was doomed by Tyrese Haliburton's ruptured Achilles. They punted on the next campaign and thought, with their point guard's return, everything would return normal; that the half-court offense would run smooth as a "picket fence;" and that the jumpers would automatically fall like the ones by Jimmy Chitwood. But Haliburton (unfortunately) isn't as quiet nor as down to earth as Mr. Chitwood, nor is an NBA season comparable to a Hollywood movie. It's a lot more complicated than that.

The Pacers are finding that out the hard way. It only took testy Tyrese a handful of games until he got injured again (it's a hamstring now) and his team sorely misses the All Star as their 4-8 record puts them in the lower region of the Eastern Conference. On Friday against the New York Knicks, Haliburton will also be reduced to trolling from the sidelines, and he won't be able to right the wrongs that have plagued his squad so far this season. It's a huge advantage for Mike Brown's men as Haliburton is the straw that stirs the Apple Cider for Indiana. Most of their strategy revolves around his talent, his scoring and his distribution.

But it's not like the Pacers are just struggling out of the gate because Hali has only played four games. Even with him they lost to Milwaukee, barely got by New Orleans and were annihilated by Dallas. Indiana can't shoot threes (just over 33 percent), despite a traditionally fast pace their offense isn't even close to functioning well (20th in offensive rating), and their D is nothing to write home about either (15th in the league). The Pacers are an atrocious rebounding team (29th in rebounding percentage) and they struggle mightily to hit their free throws.

Do they do anything well? They do, in fact, take good care of the ball and have a one of the best assist-to-turnover-ratios in the entire Association. Individually Pascal Siakam is enjoying a strong start to the season (22.7 points) and Andrew Nembhard has come firing out of the gates as well (17.1 points, 42.9 percent from downtown). A lot of guys are still left from that Finals team two years ago, so in theory they should know what it takes. And Rick Carlisle is still a tremendously respected coach, at least when he isn't whining like a five year old who didn't get his wish at Toys-R-Us.

The skipper really has his work cut out for him early in the year. New center Ivica Zubac, a classic, plodding back-to-basket big man with considerable post skills, hasn't really found his place in an adjusted Pacers system that once was predicated on playing five-out at all costs. Continuity has also been a big issue as Carlisle has already started 11 different players, at times making it seem like the Pacers are still on their tanking ways from last year. They obviously aren't and it should be noted that slow starts are nothing new for Indiana under Carlisle. Consider yourself warned.

With Friday's game being another Cup contest you can bet that the Pacers will be highly motivated and our beloved Knicks better be ready for a hostile environment. Keep calm if Haliburton tries some stupid sideline voodoo as he certainly will when he senses a camera being on him. Feed Karl-Anthony Towns in a favorable matchup for him, let Brunson do his thing against Indiana's supposed Brunson stoppers who always seem to just limit him to 35. Make use of your more talented depth, outshoot the struggling shooting team and control the tempo. Just show that you are the better team – which the Knicks, even with their current ups and downs, are at this point in the season.

And please leave the Indiana underdog stories where they belong and where they are actually fun. Leave them to Hoosiers.

Who: New York Knicks (6-5) at Indiana Pacers (4-8)Date: Friday, November 13, 2026Time: 7:00 PM ETPlace: Gainbridge Fieldhouse, Indianapolis, INTV: Prime VideoFollow: @ptknicksblog and bsky

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

#2970. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2969. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

Vinh ranks No. 123 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 28.50%. See Marvell Technology Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2968. Devin Booker’s 30 points lift Suns past Hornets, 101-96

The Phoenix Suns led 48-31 halfway through the second quarter, and it looked like they were on their way to another stress-free win against a depleted Charlotte Hornets team. Without the franchise cornerstone players Kon Knueppel and Brandon Miller, and with a superb stretch from the Suns bench, the Hornets looked dead in the water, but ultimately fought back and made this game a slog for the final two and a half quarters for the Suns, who won 101-96.

Two things were apparent in this game: the Phoenix Suns could not rebound, and the Charlotte Hornets could not stop turning the basketball over. The Hornets outrebounded the Suns 64-to-43 and outscored the Suns 16-7 in second-chance points. The Suns forced 27 Hornets turnovers (each Hornets starter had at least four turnovers) while the Suns committed eight turnovers. So the rebounding numbers are skewed in favor of the Hornets because they had 13 fewer shots on goal than the Suns did. However, 22 Suns defensive rebounds compared to 15 Hornets offensive rebounds is not getting the job done at all for a Suns team that can be much better than they have been this season at rebounding.

Because the Suns could not rebound the ball and play as fast as they needed to get the offense going, the game became a half-court slog in the second half. The Suns shot 13-for-42 from the 3-point line, and a lot of the looks that went in on Tuesday did not fall for the Suns against the Hornets. Overall, the Suns were 41% from the field, and only one player had a field goal percentage higher than 50%. Devin Armani Booker scored 30 points on 12-of-20 shooting, 4-for-8 from the 3-point line, and had seven assists with just two turnovers. He made every big shot for Phoenix in clutch time, including a deep dagger 3 with 23 seconds left to give the Suns a decisive six-point lead.

All eyes were on Miles Bridges, Grayson Allen, and Royce O'Neale in this game because of the Bridges trade that happened this offseason. Bridges scored 11 points, grabbed six rebounds, and had three assists, and was effective playing with the bench units, but continued to struggle to make an impact with the starting group. Allen scored 14 points, while O'Neale scored 7 points, all coming in the fourth quarter. The Hornets were led by Coby White, who scored 21 points but did not play in the fourth quarter due to what appeared to be a minutes restriction. That certainly helped the Suns win this game, because White was getting wherever he wanted against the Suns' defense.

A win is a win, but the Suns' starting unit continued to not find any type of consistency in this game. The best stretches of basketball continue to come from this team at the beginning of the second and fourth quarters when there are at least four bench players in. The Suns closed the game with a Booker-Luke Kennard-Jordan Goodwin-Dillon Brooks-Oso Ighodaro lineup, while Jalen Green played just 21 minutes and none in the final quarter.

Green's continuing to show growth in the areas the Suns need him to grow in, but the fit with everyone else on the roster continues to be a problem, and the trust in him is obviously dwindling as he continues to not see minutes in fourth quarters. The Suns' record is now 5-6, and sometime in the near future they are going to have to change the starting lineup if it continues to look this disconnected.

All eyes were on Miles Bridges, Grayson Allen, and Royce O'Neale to start this game, and it was Grayson Allen who got things going for the Hornets early, connecting on a pair of triples. The Suns' defense lacked focus and gave up multiple wide-open layups and threes early, with Coby White hitting a wide-open 3 in semi-transition to go up 15-10. Jordan Ott called a timeout and subbed out Bridges for Ryan Dunn. The Suns' energy immediately changed on that end of the floor, as they quickly jumped on the Hornets defensively, leading to a 7-0 run engineered by Jalen Green knocking down two free throws and a corner 3 off of Suns stops.

There were two big takeaways from the Suns' first quarter: defense forced 10 turnovers and Devin Booker's pull-up 3-point shooting. Booker was dialed in, scoring 13 points on 3-of-4 shooting from 3. His comfort level shooting from long range continues to grow, and it is opening up everything for the rest of his game. The Suns led by six to end the first quarter because the Hornets did just enough on the offensive glass to stay in it, down 28-22 after one.

The Suns bench took over in the second quarter because of their hustle and tenacity on both ends of the floor. Jordan Goodwin forced multiple turnovers, leading to a wide-open dunk for Bridges and free throws off of an excellent out-of-bounds play. Collin Gillespie got free for multiple long-range bombs that went in as the Suns pushed their six-point lead out to 15 four minutes into the second quarter, 46-31.

The Suns led by as much as 17 before putting the starters back in, but there was some slippage. The Hornets went on a 14-2 run to cut the Suns' lead to 50-43. A couple of defensive breakdowns and Bridges fouling Grant Williams on a 3-point shot, leading to a 4-point play, were the catalysts for the Hornets getting back into the game. Williams scored 9 points in the second period, and another Allen 3 got the Hornets' deficit down to five at halftime, 55-50.

Even though the Suns were out-rebounded 28-to-15, the Suns still won the possession battle because the Hornets had 15 turnovers compared to the Suns' three total turnovers in the first half. The two worst stretches of the game came when the majority of the Suns' starters were on the floor, as they continued to get in their own way of being a good team.

The Suns' sloppiness from the starters continued to start the second half. As the Hornets jumped out to a 12-to-4 run to start the third quarter, the Suns' poor offensive flow led to easy shots and even more offensive rebounds for the Hornets, who led 62-59 with 8:22 left to play in the third. Since the Suns' 17-point second-quarter lead, the Suns were outscored 31-to-11 across both halves, and Coby Whites 19 points up to that point were a big reason why.

For the rest of the quarter, the Suns started to play better once Dunn, Gillespie and Maluach checked in for Ighodaro, Bridges and Green. But the Hornets responded immediately after two tough Booker midrange buckets. Both teams finished the quarter cold from the field, but Christian Anderson's solid play off the bench for the Hornets put the Hornets up 76-72 after three quarters. The Suns continued to look allergic to rebounding and playing consistent, mistake-free defense, and now a game that was once in cruise control completely slipped away from them. At no point should Jalen Green ever lead the Suns in rebounding with four total rebounds after three quarters.

It wasn't the prettiest 11-3 run ever to start the fourth quarter, but the Suns bench just never relents on their pressure. It started with the Suns grabbing a defensive rebound and a Bridges dunk in transition. The Suns attacked the rim and glass early in the quarter, capped off by a Gillespie midrange pull-up after a travel from Reid. Next play, the Suns force a turnover, Gillespie misses another midrange shot, but Goodwin chases down the rebound and lobs it up to Ighodaro to give the Suns an 83-79 lead.

The Suns continued to string together good possessions for the most part but could not create much separation. Luke Kennard scored multiple midrange shots, and a Gillespie drive-and-dump off to Maluach should have ended in a dunk, but he mishandled it, and O'Neale sprayed in a triple on the other end.

Booker, Brooks, and Ighodaro checked and left Goodwin and Kennard in. After Williams got open for a layup, Booker found Goodwin for an open 3 that just rimmed out. Booker connected on a midrange jump shot to give the Suns a three-point lead. The Suns continued to get multiple stops, and finally a Brooks 3-point shot gave the Suns a 92-86 lead.

The Suns and Hornets traded multiple empty possessions until Reid broke the seal with a 3 of his own, but Booker responded with a tough floater in the lane to get the Suns back up five with one minute to play. A cutting Diabate layup on the other end got it right back to a one-possession game with the Suns up 94-91. Then it was Devin Booker time as he hit a deep, deep 3-point pull with 23 seconds left to give Phoenix a decisive six-point lead. That is, until Brooks fouled O'Neale on a 3-point shot, which, of course, went in for the four-point play.

The Suns inbounded the basketball to Booker. He quickly passed it back to Bridges, who was fouled by Allen, and he made both free throws. Then Booker poetically stole an Allen pass for the Hornets' 27th turnover of the night, and sank both free throws as the Suns went on to win 101-96.

The Phoenix Suns finish up their homestand with the Los Angeles Lakers on Sunday afternoon.

#2961. Lakers' Luka Dončić 'feeling good' after injury scare, per report

Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Dončić had an injury scare against the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday, Nov. 11.

Dončić suffered a right ankle injury with 4:25 left in the game after landing on Kyle Kuzma while attempting a shot. The point guard was awarded a foul and attempted his free throws before leaving for the locker room.

Coach JJ Redick stated after the game that Dončić had an ankle "tweak."

"I'm good," said Dončić, who finished with 32 points. "Obviously painful a little bit, but I'll be fine."

ESPN's Shams Charania reported Thursday that Dončić "actually woke up with no pain.

"He's feeling good. He will go through his normal treatment process," Charania said.

Dončić is expected to play against the Phoenix Suns in the Lakers' next game on Nov. 15.

#2954. Wolves 133, Mavericks 94: You Like That?

Do you remember the last time the Minnesota Timberwolves played basketball?

Since the Wolves last played, the Minnesota football team got flat-out embarrassed on national television, then went through all five stages of grief before building a time machine that sent them back to the days of Kirk Cousins, and now, in one fell swoop, has gone from pumpkins to the NFL's new belle of the ball.

And somewhere in the midst of all of this, Joan Beringer is officially no longer a teenager and is starting to go gray.

I'm not saying having no Wolves games is responsible for any of this, or that if we had played sooner, maybe Beringer wouldn't have had to have a birthday (is that how that works?). I'm not saying that! But man, does it feel good to finally have this team back on the court and on our screens again.

In their first game back in eons, the Wolves laced up and faced off against a team in the Dallas Mavericks that seems to be fast-tracking a soft rebuild. On paper, both teams are pretty evenly matched in several categories, namely FG% (DAL #4, MIN #5), turnovers (DAL #10, MIN #11), and steals (DAL #14, MIN #15), so the Wolves needed to win the possession battle.

Certainly, the Mavericks would be up for the challenge, so it would stand to reason that this would be a challenging game for the Wolves. Right?

To start the game, Jonathan Kuminga was on a torrid pace, muscling to the basket for a lay-up and cutting to the rim for a dunk off a beautiful find by LaMelo Ball, who proceeded to find Jaden McDaniels on a similar play a few possessions later.

The Mavericks settled in and muddied the game as the quarter went on. At the 5:16 mark, TJ Shannon subbed in, and PJ Washington proceeded to bully him to the rim for an and-one. This was a great quarter for Washington, as he finished with 10 points in the quarter.

Perhaps an accurate distillation of the rest of the game, though, is that those 10 points would be it for Washington, and the Mavs 24-19 lead would soon be relinquished and gone for good.

Ayo Dosunmu and the bench mob of Bones Hyland, Jaylen Clark, Trey Lyles, and Beringer burst the Wolves out of the mud like the Snoats brothers in "Raising Arizona", spurring an 11-0 run that got the crowd dancing on their feet. After a couple of free throws by Naji Marshall to stop the run, a Gobert offensive rebound off a missed Dosunmu corner three allowed the Wolves to close out the quarter with this Clark nuke.

The second quarter saw more of the same for the Wolves. After a sloppy turnover by Dosunmu led to a corner three by Zaccharie Risacher, the Wolves had given up 15 points off of six turnovers. Those numbers would balloon to 25 and 11 by the end of the first half, and it wouldn't matter.

Dosunmu continues to truck along as Mr. Consistency, a perfect cog in the absence of Anthony Edwards. He finished the half with 12 points by doing what he does best: being an absolute bully out on the break.

As for Ball, he was unconscious from deep, and finding cutters left and right. Much has been said about his fit with Gobert, and there were still a couple of miscues on the Ball-Gobert feed tonight. Near the end of the half, though, Ball slipped into the paint as the ball guided him around. He faked once, and then floated the ball up for a Gobert slam.

It was a thing of beauty, and exactly why sticking to the process matters.

Going into this game, Ball's assist-to-turnover ratio (1.62) wasn't pretty to look at, but for the most part, I don't mind it.

The way I see it is that they are mostly a statistical fallout of process-driven stuff that should temper with time. Some of the results might be an eyesore for box score perusers, but I love the process. I love that Ball is not afraid to continue to funnel the ball into Rudy, even if his individual numbers risk taking a hit. The box score won't tell the whole story. It seldom does. But Ball's commitment to the process should pay dividends as the season bears itself out.

The Wolves would go into halftime leading 67-52. And then…

Ball continued his unconscious streak from long-range, followed by a Dosunmu transition trey. Dusty May would call a timeout not even two minutes into the second half to try to stop the bleeding. That worked just as well as trying to stop a Beringer nosebleed.

The Wolves were playing loose and fast. McDaniels was gliding down the court for barely contested lay-ins. Kuminga was hitting threes, and the lead would soar to 32 points midway through the third after McDaniels was blocked, got the ball back, and then threw down a vicious slam.

Right before the McDaniels sequence, Beringer re-entered the game with a clear mask (!). Before the mask change, Beringer had just one point and zero blocks. After the mask change: 12 points and three blocks. Need I say more? That new mask is magic.

Although there were times through the third where it looked more like a horse race at Canterbury, with players flying around with their manes on fire, any and all sloppy play was a moot point, and the Mavericks would end up waving the white flag before the third quarter had even concluded.

The fourth quarter was mostly a non-event because of the score (Wolves 103 – Mavericks 69 at the end of the third), outside of a Gobert and-one off a Kuminga miss (where he converted on the free throw!) and a smooth jump hook by the magic-masked-man Beringer, but I wanted to shout out Isaiah Evans on his first official points in the NBA.

I know it was on a goal-tend, but still, a milestone is a milestone.

And a shout-out to Jon "Boog" Sciambi on the call tonight, his first Wolves game of the season. I know he is beloved in Wrigleyville as the play-by-play announcer for the Chicago Cubs. As a Twins fan, I hold no ill will towards the Cubs. It's that other team on the South Side that I have problems with.

In the end, the Wolves were able to completely stifle Cooper Flagg for 12 points on just 5-12 shooting. Seeing the Wolves, he was probably having nightmares about his last game against them, when he went 5-19 for 12 points. I guess this game was an improvement? Baby steps, teenager. Baby steps.

The Wolves were also able to silence Kyrie Irving, who up until now had looked rejuvenated and was outperforming his career shooting splits across the board.

Caleb Martin just about doubled his scoring average (seven points tonight). He should be proud of that.

But this was perhaps the Wolves' best all-around effort in this season's infancy. Off a long break against a good team, Target Center was rocking, and from start to finish, it was just a rollicking good time.

Right now, the Mavericks have 13 guys logging double-digit minutes. It's clear that Dusty May and his coaching staff have spent the early part of this season seeing what works and what doesn't. I'd expect the rotations to get tighter as the season wears on, as players fill out their roles and the overall numbers flesh out into something a bit more comprehensive. So far, though, May has struck a good balance of thinking big picture while still keeping his team competitive on a nightly basis.

In a fairly perforated coaching pipeline, May's seamless transition from college to the NBA has been nothing short of impressive. I was late to the magic of May, only first hearing about him last year as the coach of the eventual national champion Michigan Wolverines. Chalk it up to object permanence on my part, but I know who he is now, and it's almost as if he was built in a lab to know ball and coach ball.

Yesterday also marked the one-year anniversary of the Mavericks dumping disgraced GM and possible secret Los Angeles Lakers operative Nico Harrison. Going from Harrison to Masai Ujiri seems to have stabilized this organization. Finally, adults have returned to the table.

Enmities from the 2024 WCF aside, I'm happy for Mavericks fans.

The Timberwolves continue their eight-game homestand on Saturday, as Chicago O'Hare International Airport's Bulls fly into Target Center. O'Hare ranks first as the busiest airfield in the world. Meanwhile, their team ranks first in a bunch of categories you'd rather not rank first in. Liftoff is at 7:00 PM CT on DAZN-MN and NBA League Pass.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2952. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2949. Clippers' Bradley Beal still sidelined, getting 'not good results' in ramp-up

While the Los Angeles Clippers seem to be seeing strides made by some of their other injured players, Bradley Beal doesn't appear to be on the same trajectory.

Beal is exactly one year removed from having undergone season-ending surgery to repair his fractured hip. The three-time All-Star's hip has been cleared, but Beal remains on the injury report with right knee inflammation.

Beal has continued to serve as "a veteran leader" who spends time talking with the young guys, but when he will be able to get back on the court remains undecided.

"Every time we get him on the court to do something three days in a row, then it's like, it's not good results," Clippers coach Tyronn Lue told reporters on Nov. 12.

Beal averaged just 8.2 points, 1.7 assists, and 0.8 rebounds in his six games played last season before his injury.

For his career, Beal has averaged 21.4 points, 4.3 assists and 4.0 rebounds per game through 14 seasons.

Beal re-signed with the Clippers as a free agent in August to a two-year deal worth $13.2 million. The deal has a player option for the second year.
