#2916. Scouting New York Jets offensive tackle Blake Freeland

The Jets recently signed former Colts offensive lineman Blake Freeland to their active roster from the Bengals' practice squad. Today, we break him down in detail.

The 25-year old Freeland is listed at 6'7" and 302 pounds and was a fourth round pick out of BYU in 2023. He has started nine games in his career, all of which were for the Colts in his rookie year.

Freeland was a three-star high school recruit but headed to BYU with zero experience of playing on the offensive line having been a quarterback for two years before moving to tight end and defensive end as a senior. He also played basketball and represented his school in shot put and javelin throwing.

On his arrival at BYU, Freeland converted to offensive tackle but didn't see the field until the middle of his freshman season. However, he ended up starting the last seven games and held up reasonably well, as he only allowed one sack.

He didn't begin his sophomore year as a starter, but ended up making a total of eight starts in 2020, during which he showed promise by not allowing a sack all year.

In 2021 and 2022, Freeland started all 26 games and was twice named to Phil Steele's all-Independent Conference first team. He was also a third team AP all-American in 2022.

Heading into the 2023 pre-draft process, Freeland was being touted as a possible day two pick. However, his rawness was exposed during Senior Bowl week. Even so, he managed to stablize his stock with good workouts at the combine and his pro day.

The Colts eventually drafted Freeland in the fourth round and he started off his rookie year on the bench. He soon made his first start due to a week four injury, and by the end of the year had started nine games.

In 2024, he failed to earn a starting role and spent the first half of the season as a healthy scratch. He played in the last eight games, although he only played 23 offensive snaps, 22 of which were in the season finale.

In year three, a serious leg injury during preseason forced Freeland to spend the entire year on injured reserve and then he faced an uphill battle to make the team out of training camp this summer.

After being released in final cuts following preseason, Freeland signed to Cincinnati's practice squad before being poached by the Jets last month following Max Mitchell's concussion.

Let's move onto some more in-depth analysis of what Freeland brings to the table, based on in-depth research and film study.

Freeland is extremely tall, with excellent length, but was not strong enough when he entered the league so has no doubt been trying to add weight and is almost certainly heavier than his listed weight of 302. He managed 25 bench press reps at his pro day.

The rest of his workout numbers are impressive as he ran the 40-yard dash in under five seconds at the combine, then broke the combine record for offensive linemen with a 37-inch vertical jump. His broad jump of 120 inches was also impressive.

Having posted underwhelming agility numbers at the combine, Freeland did those drills again at his pro day and improved them dramatically.

Freeland has extensive experience of playing at both tackle positions. He started two seasons of each in college and has played both during his preseason appearances. At the NFL level, his first two starts were on the left but the other seven were on the right.

He would not be suitable for an interior role and has never played there at any level.

Freeland only allowed two sacks in his entire college career, with a low pressure rate. However, BYU's system operates with a lot of screens and RPO's so he didn't play as many true pass sets as you'd like to see from an incoming prospect.

Not surprisingly, he struggled during his nine starts as a rookie, giving up four sacks.

Talented NFL level players were always going to be able to exploit his lack of experience, and it didn't help his confidence that he faced Aaron Donald in his first NFL start.

Despite his athletic numbers, Freeland can sometimes be slow to get into his stance and susceptible to being beaten by speed rushers around the edge.

However, his biggest concern is his anchor. He can really struggle to repel bull rushers and speed-to-power moves.

Freeland's run blocking grades according to Pro Football Focus were very good when he was in college, but he was less consistent at the NFL level. The 2023 season when Freeland played extensively for the Colts was Jonathan Taylor's least productive of his career and he only had one 100-yard game which came on a day when Freeland didn't play.

Once again, play strength is an issue here, as he will get stood up on the edge and allow his man to fight off his block in the trenches.

He does give a good effort though, and his athletic ability means he can get out in space to good effect.

Freeland had some decent moments on short yardage plays, even though he's not much of a drive blocker. It may suit him that effort is arguably more important than technique in such situations.

Freeland obviously has the athleticism to get out in front of a screen and, as noted, it was something BYU did regularly when he was there.

Here's a play where he was at right tackle. On this one, rather than leaking out, he has an initial responsibility to prevent the upfield rush but then finds someone to block so the play doesn't get bottled up.

Considering his speed and agility numbers, it is disappointing to see that Freeland is sometimes stiff and mechanical in his movements.

One other obvious area that needs work is his hand placement. He can be slow to get his hands up and defensive players will take advantage of this.

His other big issue is pad level. This was always going to be an issue for someone as tall as him and he can tend to play with a narrow base so is often overpowered.

Freeland had seven penalties in seven games as a freshman but improved his on-field discipline over the course of his college career with just nine penalties in the last three seasons.

At the NFL level, he's been flagged six times. Four of these were false starts and two were for illegal man downfield. He has zero holding penalties.

Freeland's only role on special teams in college was as a blocker on the placekicking unit and that has been the case in the NFL too. He has had no issues or penalties within this role.

Freeland displayed good patience on stunts as a college player, and has shown he can handle those at the NFL level too.

However, it's in those one-on-one battles – especially as a pass rusher – where his lack of experience can be detrimental as he can be slow to react to or anticipate counters.

Freeland is a player who is known for his toughness and a relentless work ethic. He also has good leadership traits, having been a three-time high school captain – he played two years as a quarterback, remember – and also a captain at BYU.

To miss your entire third season having entered the NFL as someone who was considered a raw developmental prospect is rough luck and Freeland's fractured leg in 2025 has obviously hurt his career.

Prior to this, injuries weren't an issue as he played in 44 games in his college career despite being a healthy scratch for the first seven weeks.

As noted, BYU's scheme used a lot of screens and RPO's and, as such, probably wasn't ideal preparation for entering the NFL. However, Freeland has since had four camps with the Colts and spent a couple of months in Cincinnati this year.

Although current Jets offensive coordinator Frank Reich left the Colts in 2022 and Freeland was drafted by them the following season, there would presumably have been some overlap between his scheme and that of the incoming offensive coordinator Jim Bob Cooter, given that most of the key personnel was the same, so the Jets should have some concepts he's familiar with.

He was a teammate of current Jets cornerback Mory Bamba at BYU and his fellow practice squad members Jimmy Morrissey and Samuel Womack with the Colts.

The Jets picked up Freeland after Mitchell's concussion forced him to go on injured reserve. This meant that they only had three healthy tackles on their active roster, causing them to have to elevate Brant Banks from the practice squad a few times.

Once Freeland is up to speed, he'll likely be active on gameday and could eventually challenge Chukwuma Okorafor to be the swing tackle, given how unimpressive Okorafor has been seen leaving the Steelers. One of them might need to be cut when Mitchell can return, though.

Jets fans will, of course, hope that Armand Membou and Olu Fashanu remain healthy, in which case Freeland wouldn't get a chance to play. However, he could still be brought back as a project player next year.

#2914. NHL’s next TV deal will test the league’s strength while media companies prepare for NFL rights increase

One of the biggest developing stories in entertainment and sports is how the world's biggest media companies judge the value of non-NFL rights. The NHL will be the first public test into this new era of media valuation.

The NHL can begin formally negotiating a new media rights deal with its incumbent partners — Disney/ESPN and Skydance, the new owner of TNT Sports — on Jan. 1. The league wants an increase in overall rights fees between 50% and 100% over its current deal, according to sources familiar with the matter. The NHL inked a 7-year, $4.5 billion deal with ESPN and TNT Sports in 2021.

Whether the league actually gets that type of increase is still unclear — and that will be one of the most watched data points in the world of sports business next year.

National media rights for the NHL currently make up about 13% of its annual revenue, which is around $8 billion, according to CNBC estimates.

Disney/ESPN and Skydance want to renew a deal with the league, according to people familiar with the matter that asked not to be named because the discussions are private. Both companies have engaged in preliminary conversations with the league in the past few weeks, and while there's still work to do in terms of price, there's mutual interest in getting something done.

In its current deal, ESPN has access to 100 exclusive games for the 2026-27 season. Fifty-three of them air on ESPN or ABC, and the other 47 exclusive stream on ESPN. Under current terms, the league's playoffs are split between Disney and TNT Sports.

Disney is particularly interested in acquiring the rights to more playoff games, one of the people said. The NHL had its most-watched playoffs on record last season, averaging 1.8 million viewers per game. The Stanley Cup averaged 5.2 million viewers — the largest audience since 2019. It was played between the Carolina Hurricanes and the Vegas Golden Knights — not exactly marquee NHL franchises.

Last year's regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from a year ago — the highest average in more than a decade.

The sport is also having a moment culturally. Earlier this year, HBO Max said its hockey-romance series "Heated Rivalry" averaged a whopping 10.6 million viewers per episode.

International hockey competitions, such as the league's annual "4 Nations Face-Off" midseason tournament and the 2026 Winter Olympics, have drawn huge ratings. The last "4 Nations Face-Off" game — 2025's U.S. vs. Canada final — drew 9.3 million viewers.

This year's Gold Medal men's hockey game between the U.S. and Canada averaged a stunning 26 million viewers on NBC and Peacock in the U.S., according to Nielsen data. That made it the most-watched sporting event in U.S. history with a start time before 9 a.m. ET.

"We think we've got the best content," NHL Commissioner Gary Bettman said on CNBC last month. "Our ratings have continued to increase. We were up dramatically into the double digits last year. So we view ourselves coming into the marketplace in a very strong position. Having said that, the marketplace is extremely competitive and extremely fragmented. But, I believe that with fierce competition that's going on between traditional television and the newer media platforms that there'll be a great opportunity for us to both expand our reach and get paid what we think is an appropriate reflection of our value."

Recent history suggests the NHL will get the rights increase it wants. The NBA nearly tripled its national media rights revenue, signing an 11-year, $77 billion contract with Disney, Amazon and NBCUniversal in a deal that started last season.

The NHL also renewed its TV rights deal in Canada just last year with Rogers Communications, scoring an increase of more than 100% annually when adjusting for currency fluctuations.

But recent history may not match up to present reality.

While Skydance is interested in getting a deal done, the company's top priorities are paying down debt and saving money to pay for what's going to be a huge NFL rights increase — potentially $1 billion per season or more, two of the people said.

Skydance is dead set on renewing the NFL, said one of the people. Those talks will likely occur in 2028, as the league has an opt-out clause at the end of the 2029-30 season.

Now that Paramount and WBD have been pushed together, the NHL isn't as important to Skydance for distribution leverage, which already has premier sports media rights such as the NFL, The Masters, college football, March Madness, UFC and UEFA Champions League.

That's not to say the NHL isn't a priority for Skydance. Skydance still needs programming for the cable networks it owns to keep that business chugging along. But relative to WBD, it's not as much of a must-have.

"As it relates to future rights, we'll be disciplined," Skydance CEO David Ellison said during an earnings conference call this week — perhaps hinting at his company dropping not only NHL but also other rights from the WBD portfolio in the coming months and years. "Every right has to earn its place."

Perhaps Skydance remains a partner but buys fewer NHL games or sublicenses some of them. This potentially opens the door to new bidders. CNBC's own parent company Versant would be interested in a slice of NHL games. Amazon sniffs around just about every national sports property that comes to market.

Netflix? Regular season NHL games are certainly not events. Fox? NBCUniversal? Both companies need to worry about how much it's going to cost to renew the NFL. A big package of NHL games may not fit the strategy.

How much the NHL can extract from its media partners will likely set the bar for other media rights set to come to market, including MLB, which could be delayed by labor negotiations.

Disclosure: Versant Media is the parent company of CNBC.

#2910. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2908. Lendeborg, Podziemski set to give Warriors a glimpse at post-Steph Curry era

Lendeborg, Podziemski set to give Warriors a glimpse at post-Steph Curry era originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

For those curious about where the Warriors will stand once Stephen Curry hangs up his sneakers, the preview has arrived.

With Curry out for several weeks, the only two players already identified as members of the post-Steph era will be front and center. The extended auditions of Yaxel Lendeborg and Brandin Podziemski begin Friday night in San Antonio against the imposing Spurs.

Lendeborg, 24, and Podziemski, 23, have done well with Curry looking over their respective shoulders, but they're now on their own. If the Warriors are to avoid a disastrous slide, they will need sufficient contributions across the roster, beginning with veterans Draymond Green and De'Anthony Melton, assuming he replaces Curry in the starting lineup.

But the main characters are Lendeborg and Podziemski. They will have the ball in their hands as much or more than Green, but they produce much more scoring. Lendeborg is second on the team in scoring behind Curry, and Podziemski is third. Podziemski is second in assists behind Green, and Lendeborg is fourth. Lendeborg is first in rebounding, and Podziemski is third.

The most telling statistical category is minutes per game, because it implies an inherent trust from the coaching staff and value within the franchise. Podziemski leads all Warriors, averaging 33.4 per game, and Lendeborg is second at 32.3.

Podziemski's $110 million contract extension suggests the franchise considers him a keeper. This lavish commitment is no guarantee – see Poole, Jordan – that Podziemski will be a Warrior in 2028, much less 2031. Yet his is only the second large long-term rookie extension from Golden State since Klay Thompson received a four-year commitment in 2014.

So, yes, the Warriors believe Podziemski is as a keeper. At least for now.

"We think Brandin is a hell of a young player, a big part of our future, and he secures his future financially," coach Steve Kerr said last month after the extension was official. "And it's a deal that makes a lot of sense based on our numbers."

Those numbers relate mostly to Podziemski's positive impact on the court. He led the team with a plus-264 plus/minus as a rookie in 2023-24, ranked third on the team in his second season, dipped to eighth last season but this season ranks behind only Curry.

When Curry was sidelined for two months last season, Podziemski's scoring ticked up 2.2 points per game, but his efficiency tumbled, as he posted splits of 44.8/34.8 percent. He's at 48.1/39.7 percent this season.

The question before Podziemski now is whether he is better equipped to maintain similar scoring efficiency without the space afforded by Curry's famous gravity. And can the fourth-year guard improve his 69-percent free-throw shooting?

The question before Lendeborg is whether he can handle the burden that comes with rising from No. 2 on the scouting report to No. 1. It's a lot for anyone unaccustomed to it, perhaps even more for a rookie. The spotlight now is much brighter than any he has seen. He felt it after Curry left the game Wednesday night in Houston and responded with a game-high 25 points and team-high 10 rebounds.

"He really took a lot of the offense on his shoulders, and he's obviously very capable," Kerr told reporters at Toyota Center.

Lendeborg in his first 12 NBA games has passed, if not aced, every offensive test thrown his way so far. It's a thin resumé, but it already is delighting the Warriors and their fans, who are falling in love with his potential.

Kerr was quick to place the ultimate faith in Lendeborg's ability, elevating him to primary offensive engine when Curry is off the floor. Lendeborg's response has been 21.7 points per game on 51.8/42.6 shooting splits, while adding 7.4 rebounds and 3.7 assists. His Rookie of the Year candidacy is strong.

A lot must happen for the Warriors to win at least half their games without Curry. Green must take and make more shots. Melton, shooting 42.9 percent from deep, must stay close to that level. Georges Niang and Al Horford must produce off the bench. Charles Bassey must remain a force in the paint. Brandon Williams must return soon.

"We have to keep working on our offensive execution," Kerr said in Houston. "… Without Steph, we have to be organized in our concepts and with our spacing so that we can attack and create shots for each other."

Much of that task will fall on Lendeborg and Podziemski. If they handle it well, the Warriors can be respectable without their superstar.

#2906. Giants-Commanders: 5 plays that led to the loss

The New York Giants lost a close home game to the Washington Commanders, 24-21. New York's defense forced two interceptions in the fourth quarter — with a four-point lead— but the Giants' offense went three-and-out on both drives, resulting in an opportunistic Washington score that secured the win for the Commanders.

John Harbaugh used the apt word "disappointing" to describe the loss. New York was on the precipice of a commanding lead in the NFC East, but Washington now owns the tiebreaker between the two teams, even though New York leads the standings by two games. Losing home games in the division is tough, and something that's very familiar to Giants fans, unfortunately.

Still, the Giants do lead the division and have a tough stretch of opponents remaining on their schedule. They have two home games against the Jaguars and the 49ers in the next three weeks (a game against the Colts in between), as well as a trip out to Seattle. This season is FAR from over, but a win against Washington would have been a welcome, comfortable result. Here are five plays that led to the loss.

This was a three-play sequence in the fourth quarter where EACH side of the football failed miserably. Firstly, the defense shouldn't bear much blame for this loss; they forced two interceptions and a turnover on downs in the fourth quarter. It's the offense that couldn't pick up a yard and was stopped multiple times on third down when it mattered most, but I digress back to my point…

The third-and-3 quarterback sweep where Sonny Styles tackled J.J. McCarthy for a loss of two was the first of this terrible sequence:

Ar'Darius Washington intercepted Daniels with 2:19 left on the clock; 29 seconds later, the Giants are punting after three offensive plays for the second consecutive drive after an interception. I don't like the call at all, but the failed third-down conversion attempt was made much worse by a rare terrible moment from punter Jordan Stout.

Stout punted the football 12 YARDs from Washington's 41-yard line to their 29 — an inexcusable mistake. New York couldn't consider a 58-yard field goal attempt from Dominic Zvada, who missed a 33-yarder on New York's second drive, so special teams' unreliability compounded the problem, and this punt gave Washington solid field position.

The third play, though, WAS on the defense. Tae Banks committed a defensive pass interference against Treylon Burks, which gave Washington the ball at the New York 38-yard line with a minute and a half to go in the 21-17 Giants' game. Two plays later, the game was 24-21, Washington.

The Giants forced a third-and-4 with just over a minute left in the game, and New York rushed seven, initially, dropping two defensive linemen off a half-second after the snap. Jaydon Daniels threw it right past the Giants' defensive lineman to Stefon Diggs (3), who had inside leverage against Dru Phillips (22). Phillips fell and took out Tae Banks (2), allowing Diggs to walk into the end zone. The Giants capitulated the lead after several fourth-quarter plays that gave them opportunities to seal the victory. New York received the football back, though…

Washington ran Cover-0 on this third-and-10 with 33 seconds left in the game. J.J. McCarthy (18) had to heave the ball under pressure, taking a big hit in the process. New York was attempting to hit Malik Nabers (1) on the slot fade against Amik Robertson (2). Isaiah Likely (9) ran a quick curl and Malachi Fields (88) stayed on the line of scrimmage, which — in theory — would have given the Giants a true one-on-one matchup with Nabers against Robertson, but Rasual Douglas (29) had something else planned.

Douglas played off Fields with an inside turn, looking through the No.2 and No. 3 receivers (inner receivers, Nabers and Likely). Robertson read McCarthy and carried Nabers vertical, giving Robertson support on the outside. The ball was underthrown and thrown with a high trajectory, which allowed Douglas to squeeze Nabers' slot vertical; in doing so, he undercut the route and intercepted the pass.

Washington made a wise play by having Douglas play at depth and leverage to read Nabers' route and help his teammate on the vertical, despite being assigned to Fields. It's also something the Giants can learn from. Washington knew this from tape study, since New York runs the same slot-fade with the outside receiver staying put on the line of scrimmage.

The Giants had two solid offensive drives to start the game. On their first drive, New York punted from the Washington 42-yard line after taking a delay of game on a fourth-and-7 from the 37-yard line. They played the game of field position and did not attempt a 54-yard field goal. The Giants then got Zvada into a position to hit a 33-yard field goal, and the rookie kicker missed it wide left. It's just inexcusable.

TWO solid Giants' drives yield ZERO points because of the kicker, with one ending in the 33-yard miss above. John Harbaugh provided confidence to the media when discussing Zvada after the game, but these missed kicks by Giants' kickers are an all-too-familiar occurrence that is now persisting with the undrafted rookie kicker out of Michigan.

The Giants ended their first half by surrendering a nine-play, 67-yard drive that ended in this 1-yard touchdown catch by Ben Sinnott:

All three of Washington's touchdowns were on third down for Washington: the Ben Sinnott and Stefon Diggs' ones above, and the 31-yarder to Antonio Williams. The one to Sinnott, though, was in the final seconds of the first half. The Giants' defense surrendered the drive, and Washington bled the clock perfectly to not provide the Giants an opportunity to drive down the field for points. It was excellently handled by Washington.

An unfortunate bonus play makes the cut here. New York scored a touchdown on their first drive of the second half after the Giants' defense responded to the end of the first half with a three-and-out that lost 15 yards—wow! Unfortunately, Washington had an answer with a third-and-1 31-yard strike to Antonio Williams. that gave Washington a 17-14 lead.

New York, though, drove the football down the field on a five-play, 53-yard drive, with a big 16-yard catch to Malachi Fields on a second-and-seven. McCarthy then completed a pass to Nabers for three-yards on a second-and-12 at the Washington 19-yard line, and Nabers allowed the ball to get punched out of his grasp.

It was a red zone trip that ended in a turnover. Luckily the Giants' defense forced a three-and-out, but New York's offense punted the football — yet again — from Washington's territory.

#2904. CFTC Chair Selig tells CNBC crypto industry is embracing agency rules as legislation hopes fade

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission is already seeing demand from crypto exchanges planning to opt in to its newly proposed rules governing digital assets, according to Chairman Michael Selig.

The commodities regulator in October proposed rules aimed at preventing abuse in crypto markets and codifying crypto transaction rules under a federal rulebook. In the announcement, Selig said the move was a "critical step" in ensuring the United States remains the crypto capital of the world.

Speaking this week at Cantor Convergence, an annual FinTech conference held in Miami Beach, Selig told CNBC in an exclusive interview that many of the major crypto exchanges are already registered with the CFTC because they offer financial derivatives, including perpetual futures, on their platforms and would now be able to add spot trading.

"We're also getting interest from new entrants who say, 'Look, signing up with 50 different state regimes is tough. We'd rather come to one regulator and get a license at the CFTC' so those new entrants are lining up to come register with us as well," he added.

After the Clarity Act, a comprehensive market structure bill that established a regulatory framework for digital assets, stalled in the Senate in September, the CFTC and the SEC moved to establish federal frameworks using their existing statutory authorities.

"We need to protect our markets and protect our customers using those markets so we'll continue to do exactly that in the wake of the Clarity Act failing to get the votes it needed to get to the president's desk during the current Congress," Selig said.

"Our statute does provide for authority of the agencies to register exchanges that want to offer margined, leveraged, or financed crypto assets or other types of commodity trading so we're utilizing that authority to allow our existing exchanges to begin offering these products under a regulated framework," he said.

Selig added that the oversight is opt-in for exchanges that are not currently offering margin leveraged financing, which is not permitted under existing state regimes.

But the CFTC's new rules could face new scrutiny and oversight in 2027, with Democrats set to take control of the House and Senate after the midterms. Selig told CNBC he hopes the U.S. won't see a wave of software developers moving offshore, as many crypto businesses threatened to do under the Biden administration.

"I really hope that we learned lessons from the last several years in this space," he said.

Crypto firms were at odds with Washington during the Biden administration, asking for clear operating rules, while also facing lawsuits brought by the SEC under former chair Gary Gensler's leadership. That shifted after President Donald Trump's 2024 victory.

"I do hope that we can work together with both sides of the aisle to protect software developers who want to do the right thing," Selig said, adding that those looking to commit crimes and engage in fraud should be held accountable.

When asked whether he's prepared for increased scrutiny from Congress in 2027, Selig said, "Congress will do its job investigating where it needs to investigate."

"I'm happy to answer any questions that Congress has for me just as I would answer Congress' questions today as I've done before hearing committees several times already," he said.

Selig has also faced heat from Democrats for his handling of prediction markets. In February, 23 Democratic senators sent a letter to Chairman Selig urging him to reverse the agency's greenlighting of prediction markets.

Selig has argued that prediction platforms are federally regulated as more states try to rein them in. The CFTC has sued several states to defend what the agency views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate event contracts, which it classifies as swaps.

"We're going to fight this up to the Supreme Court if that's where it's going," Selig said. "Our view on the statute is that we have exclusive jurisdiction over these markets, and I will not allow for states to go bring lawsuits against our exchanges, claiming that they're unlicensed gambling."

— CNBC's Tanaya Macheel contributed to this report.

#2897. 10-year Treasury yield moves to 5.17% as traders weigh producer price report

The 10-year Treasury yield stayed over 5% on Friday after October's producer price index, a measure of wholesale prices, came in slightly less than expected.

The yield — the key benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — rose 4 basis points to 5.143%. It reached a high of 5.174% earlier in the day.

The 2-year Treasury note yield, typically more sensitive to short-term Federal Reserve interest rate decisions, was lower by less than 1 basis point at 4.859%. The longer-dated 30-year Treasury bond yield, sensitive to geopolitical risks, was more than 4 basis points higher at 5.472%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, and yields and prices move in opposite directions.

October's producer price index rose 0.3% versus September, less than the 0.4% expected by economists polled by Dow Jones. But on an annual basis, PPI rose 6.5% over the past 12 months, the biggest annual increase since November 2022.

There was "no silver lining" in the inflation report, said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at FWDBONDS, adding that core PPI — which excludes food, energy and trade services — increased 0.5% in October.

"It is economics 101 that producer prices lead consumer prices and right now it looks like consumers will be on the hook for a fresh burst of factory inflation," he said.

The moves come after 10-year Treasury yields jumped more than 13 basis points to more than 5% in Thursday's session, with the 2-year yield soaring more than 15 basis points.

Investors now see a Federal Reserve rate hike next month as a certainty. Traders are pricing in 100% odds of a rise at the Dec. 9 meeting, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool. Odds of a quarter percentage point hike now stand at 89%, with the chance of a half point increase at around 14%.

Energy inflation remains a key part of the puzzle, with oil posting its second weekly decline as prospects of renewed strikes by the U.S. against Iran fade. U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures for December delivery were down 0.2% at $88.56 per barrel. Brent crude, the international oil benchmark, was higher by 0.02% at $95.84.

#2894. Fantasy Basketball Waiver Wire: 9 NBA Sleepers & Pickups for Yahoo Leagues

Injuries continue to accumulate, meaning that for many fantasy basketball managers, the waiver wire is looking relatively shallow. However, with each new injury, there is a player inching toward a return. Getting ahead of things in fantasy basketball can be crucial when it comes to ROS success. As always, the waiver wire is the place to be, providing managers with bargain players who could be about to ascend the NBA fantasy rankings. And remember, never assume a player is rostered. It always pays to double-check, just in case they have been overlooked.

Identifying players benefiting from expanded roles, whether it's an offensive threat delivering points and threes or a defensive-minded player boosting your blocks and steals, is vital as you navigate the season.

Let's dive into nine key NBA sleepers whose current stats suggest they are poised for significant value and are currently rostered in fewer than 40% of Yahoo leagues.

Larsson has been one of the more underrated, quiet achievers this season, having also recently moved into the starting lineup for Miami. While he doesn't have the highest upside, Larsson is a perfect example of a plug-and-play fantasy asset. Through 11 appearances this season, he has averaged just over 23 fantasy points per game, which puts him in the top-100 range on most nights. Those in High Score leagues can safely deploy him, in the hope that he manages to catch fire from the field or locks down on defense. However, when all else fails, he should still do enough to provide your team with meaningful value.

Johnson continues to work his way back from an early-season ankle injury, which ultimately cost him just three games. Since getting back on the floor, Johnson has played at least 29 minutes in six straight games, scoring at least 18 points in two of his past three appearances. In that three-game period, he has been a borderline top-100 player in High Score leagues, averaging almost 26 fantasy points per contest. Not unlike Larsson, Johnson should be a relatively stable asset, although one with slightly more upside, particularly on the offensive end.

Despite the fact that the landscape in Sacramento is far from predictable, Hunter has managed to carve out a consistent role, logging at least 30 minutes in seven straight games. His overall appeal is built around an ability to score the basketball, which makes him less appealing than both Larsson and Johnson. However, he has recorded at least 20 fantasy points in four of his past six games, with two of those performances yielding 28 and 38 fantasy points. His role feels relatively secure, making him a low-upside target in standard High Score leagues.

Taking a chance on Missi should certainly be considered "getting ahead of the curve," given the ultra-small sample size we have to work with. After a breakout performance in the opening game of the season, Missi suffered a knee injury in game No. 2, which has subsequently cost him 10 straight games. However, it appears he's nearing a return, having been upgraded to doubtful for Friday's game against the Grizzlies. While he could very well remain sidelined for that game, it's reasonable to assume that he could be back as soon as next week. If you are on the hunt for rebounds and blocks, Missi should probably be high on your list of priorities.

We know the drill when it comes to Caruso's fantasy value. As a per-minute guy, he typically delivers for anyone rostering him. However, there are two main issues when it comes to his overall appeal, those being minutes and games played. Typically, the Oklahoma City coaching staff is hesitant to play him more than about 20 minutes on any given night, in an attempt to maximize his games played. With that said, injuries to other players could see Caruso doing a little more than anticipated, especially if his last outing is anything to go by. On the second night of a back-to-back, Caruso finished with 14 points, seven rebounds, three steals and four 3-pointers in 29 minutes. It's pretty simple. Pick him up, cross your fingers and hope for the best.

Known as a steals specialist, Thybulle is one of the more frequent visitors when it comes to the waiver wire. While that fact remains true, Thybulle's role this season feels a little more sustainable than in past seasons. He has played in every game for the Lakers thus far, starting all 13. His playing time has remained relatively stable, allowing him to ease into his new role without the added pressure of trying to "prove himself". He has been a borderline top-100 player to this point, averaging 2.2 steals, 0.8 blocks and 1.7 3-pointers in 24.4 minutes per game. While he is still quite clearly a specialist, a steady role should see him rostered in a lot more leagues than he is currently.

With Stephen Curry set to miss up to a month due to an oblique injury, Melton looks poised to shift into a sizeable role for the foreseeable future. To this point, it's been largely rocks and diamonds when it comes to Melton's fantasy output. Through 12 games, he has averaged 22.5 fantasy points per game, a modest number to be sure. However, assuming his playing time pushes closer to 29 minutes per game, there is scope for him to flirt with 27 or 28 fantasy points, which should see him land inside the top 100 in points formats. If he is available in your league, it makes sense to roll the dice, given his proven ability to contribute across the board.

It's been a solid start to the season for da Silva, having now scored in double digits in six of his past seven games. After being added in most leagues, a recent calf injury saw him dropped in several spots, opening the door for savvy managers to pick him up when the time is right. That time could be now, after da Silva was upgraded to questionable for Friday's game against the Bucks. If he does manage to get back on the floor, there could, of course, be some minor limitations. However, thinking beyond the next one or two games, he is someone who should be picked up in both points and category formats, at least until Paolo Banchero is back on deck.

Thomas continues to go from strength to strength, having carved out a meaningful role for himself in Cleveland. While injuries to other players have certainly helped his cause, there is a chance Thomas has simply moved ahead of certain players, a fact that shouldn't be lost on fantasy managers. He has averaged almost 32 fantasy points per game in his past two appearances, which puts him firmly on the fantasy radar. Assuming he can maintain a 25-minute-per-night role moving forward, he should at least be considered a viable addition in points leagues.

#2889. Which 49ers could be All-Pros in 2026?

As the midseason mark approaches, we're seeing some of the top award races in the NFL start to take shape, and the San Francisco 49ers definitely have a few worthy players in a couple of different spots.

Of course, there's Brock Purdy, who was the MVP frontrunner a few weeks ago and remains playing at a high level, while leading a 7-1 49ers team. San Francisco also has a number of other stars who continue to be among the NFL's best, which is why the team has regularly sent several players to the Pro Bowl every year.

But which 49ers are excelling at the top of their position to be a potential All-Pro in 2026? At the midseason mark, let's take a look at who could be in some of the award races and who could earn an All-Pro this year.

ESPN's Bill Barnwell compiled his midseason awards, which followed his quarter-season awards a few weeks back. Back then, Purdy was his No. 1 candidate for MVP. Now, he's still in the mix, but at No. 2.

"As a positive play machine, Purdy was my MVP pick after four games, having thrown one interception while taking zero sacks through one month," Barnwell wrote. "That's no longer the case, with Purdy throwing four picks and taking eight sacks over the ensuing four games, including San Francisco's first and only loss of the season at Atlanta. A bad Purdy interception with 2:42 to go and a chance to take the lead sealed it for the Falcons.

"Of course, Purdy is still running the league's lowest sack rate at 3.3%. And in terms of creating good plays, he leads the league in QBR and EPA per dropback. He's also No. 1 in success rate as a passer. And though Allen understandably gets plenty of attention as the league's most significant running quarterback, Purdy isn't far behind. He has been a valuable short-yardage option this season, with his 23 first downs as a runner ranking second behind Allen. He's third in rushing EPA behind Allen and Drake Maye.

"Purdy's numbers often get discounted by virtue of his offensive infrastructure, which is stocked with playmakers and operated by Kyle Shanahan. Though the 49ers have still had George Kittle and Christian McCaffrey on the field all season, and Mike Evans in the lineup for most of the season, Purdy deserves more of the credit for being the guy driving the offense. More than a third of his pass attempts have gone to players such as Jordan Watkins, Jacob Cowing, and Demarcus Robinson, and Purdy has made them look good in the process. Evans looked revitalized after a down season (amid injuries) in Tampa Bay in 2025. [Trevor] Lawrence and Purdy would both be legitimate MVP picks. I just lean ever so slightly toward the guy in Cincinnati being the best in football."

Joe Burrow's numbers have been ridiculous this year, and the Bengals are winning. So it makes sense that he's the MVP favorite midway through the season. But a couple of years after being on the MVP ballot, Purdy has a legitimate shot at being an All-Pro this year.

Christian McCaffrey will always be an All-Pro candidate, especially with the all-purpose spot, but it would be hard for him to earn the All-Pro honor at running back with the years Jahmyr Gibbs and Kenneth Walker are having. Those are the two Barnwell put on his midseason ballot.

However, Barnwell does have George Kittle as a first-team All-Pro at tight end, with Trey McBride coming in second. Kittle remains the best all-around tight end in football, as he does so much as a blocker. However, it feels like a part of this is because Brock Bowers was hurt for a few games earlier in the season, and with how statistics play into things, that might change by the end of the year.

Still, Kittle is firmly in that top three this year and could very well earn another All-Pro honor.

Trent Williams has looked like the best lineman in football this year, which is incredible at age 38. He was Barnwell's pick for Protector of the Year at the quarter-season mark and now at the midway point of the season as well. He's a clear contender for an All-Pro honor this year.

An underrated name at guard is Dominick Puni, who is quietly enjoying a very strong third season. He's the second-best player on one of the top offensive lines in the NFL, and has continued to ascend as one of the best right guards in the NFL. He wasn't on Barnwell's ballot, but with four guard spots on the All-Pro list, he could very well earn some votes this season.

On the defensive side, Fred Warner is a true contender for Defensive Player of the Year. Barnwell had him second behind Will Anderson, who is having a monster season for the Houston Texans. It's incredible considering he's coming off a major injury.

"I mentioned the five forced fumbles earlier, which is something we aren't supposed to see from off-ball linebackers. Those are the biggest plays Warner has made, but the future Hall of Famer has just been spectacularly efficient on a down-to-down basis," Barnwell wrote. "He has made tackles on 20% of running plays, the second-best rate among starting linebackers.

"And then, of course, there's no better linebacker in pass coverage in the league. Warner does a better job of taking away potential throws than any other linebacker, both by reading what quarterbacks are hoping to do and by running with potential receivers in coverage. His play somehow has not slipped after he dislocated his ankle a year ago. The 49ers are the league's second-best defense by QBR against play-action throws to the middle, where teams are trying to move linebackers and throw behind them for big gains. Warner is the reason those throws aren't working."

Warner is a clear All-Pro candidate and remains the best linebacker in football.

Those are all the main candidates for the 49ers, but they could realistically have six All-Pro players this season, with some fighting for bigger awards as well. Brock Purdy is in the MVP race. Trent Williams is in the Protector of the Year race. Kyle Shanahan could win Coach of the Year. Fred Warner or George Kittle could be Comeback Player of the Year after coming back from injury. And Warner could win Defensive Player of the Year.

There's been a lot of impressive performances for the 49ers this season.

#2888. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2885. Women are taking pay cuts and 'white-knuckling it' to manage menopause and work: 'I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines'

Around 2020, Amy Terry noticed she didn't feel like her usual self.

The 50-year-old Michigander was working as a director of programming at a health association when she says she began experiencing symptoms like memory loss, low energy, mental processing difficulties — or, as she describes it, "waking up every day feeling like you were slogging through mud."

"I went from being this highly competent professional person to barely being able to get through phone calls," Terry says.

As the healthcare system strained under the weight of the Covid-19 pandemic, Terry, like many other people, struggled to get care. When she finally did, she found out it wasn't Covid-19. It was perimenopause.

Terry got through a few more years juggling her symptoms with her job, but by 2023, she couldn't hold out any longer. She quit to start independent consulting.

Women, and some trans and non-binary people, often experience career setbacks stemming from menopause that can hurt them financially and professionally. Four in 10 women who have experienced menopause or perimenopause say it has negatively affected their life or work, according to a recent survey of over 3,800 U.S. women from Gallup and Melinda French Gates' Pivotal, a group of organizations focused on social progress for women and young people.

Of them, roughly 28% felt limited in the effort they could give at work, 15% decided against pursuing a promotion or leadership role, 12% reduced their hours, and 10% took an extended leave from work or didn't return.

"This isn't just a health issue," says Jasmine Escalera, career expert at resume builder site LiveCareer. "It's a workplace issue."

A 2023 Mayo Clinic study estimated menopause symptoms drove $1.8 billion in lost work time per year in the U.S. alone.

Perimenopause, the transitional period before menopause, typically begins in one's 40s and can last several years, often bringing symptoms like hot flashes, sleep issues, irritability and lower energy, according to the Office on Women's Health. Menopause typically occurs in one's 50s and marks 12 months since a person's last period, but is often used colloquially to include the preceding perimenopause as well.

The timing of menopause can create a perfect storm. It frequently comes at a time in many women's careers when they're breaking into leadership or approaching their peak earning years, which is often between the ages of 35 and 44 for women, according to data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It also typically happens when many are caregiving for aging parents or raising children. This can make for an impossible choice for many women.

"Women have fought so hard for their place in the workforce," says Mimi Wied, who began perimenopause around a decade ago. "They don't want to lose it by looking like they can't handle it."

The career consequences for women "may increase the gaps we're trying to decrease," Escalera says, referring to disparities such as those in women's pay and senior-level representation relative to men. "That is a definite talent breakdown."

For Terry, becoming an independent consultant cost her employer-sponsored healthcare at the time she most needed it. It also drastically changed her income: She says she took around an 85% pay cut in the transition.

"These are supposed to be my highest-earning years," Terry says, "and I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines."

Many women are unprepared for menopause because of social stigma around discussing it, poor health education and dismissal of women's health issues.

"I've been in the healthcare environment for 30 years; if I can't figure this out, I don't know how anyone can," says Terry.

Wied also says she didn't know what to expect. The answer quickly became apparent: The 48-year-old from Brenham, Texas, says she experienced everything from night sweats and sleeplessness to anxiety and rage.

"Sometimes I didn't feel in control of my own mouth and my body and my actions," she says.

Wied left her job as a museum docent for a secretarial role paying roughly $20,000 less when she worried her irritability would affect her capacity to help visitors, she says: "If I could have quit working, I would have."

When women do scale back at work because of menopause, they may struggle to regain that professional ground later. Terry's job switch wasn't just downshifting to a part-time role: "It's an entire change in the trajectory of your career," she says.

Factors like ageism in the job search for those of menopausal age, as well as the high cost of healthcare, particularly for common menopause treatments like hormone replacement therapy, may keep some women silently struggling through their symptoms at work.

Erin Levin is currently working through perimenopause. As a high school English teacher, the 41-year-old from San Jose, California, needs to stay on her A-game because "there is no down moment in teaching," she says.

But since starting perimenopause around two years ago, she's experienced brain fog, difficulty sleeping, and heightened anxiety and stress that make her work difficult sometimes, she says. She sometimes struggles with tasks that "were not an issue" for her a few years ago.

"Now I'm just like, God, why does it just feel so exhausting all the time?" Levin says.

Her parents help with childcare some evenings, and she tries not to schedule big tasks first thing in the morning, but it's been an adjustment. Scaling back at work isn't an option financially, she says, as a single mother sharing custody of her child.

"I still have to maintain the same appearance of having all the same skills and energy level as other people who aren't in my medical position," Levin says. "I just have to white-knuckle it."

Some women see hope in coming legislation and employer benefits offerings.

Last year, Rhode Island became the first state to enact workplace protections for women experiencing menopause. In September, Sens. Kirsten Gillibrand, D-N.Y., and Rick Scott, R-Fla., held the first congressional hearing devoted to menopause. Illinois and the city of Philadelphia have also adopted legislation taking effect in 2027 providing accommodations and protections for those in menopause.

In September, a nongovernmental organization, the International Organization for Standardization, published the first standard for developing workplace policies and practices supporting people through menstruation and menopause.

Many organizations have a ways to go in providing menopause-related benefits for their employees.

In a 2026 study from the Society for Women's Health Research and workplace menopause research organization The Fuchsia Tent, fielded among 1,100 people, around one-third of decision-makers said menopause support is currently in place or actively planned in their workplace. Fewer than 10% of employees said their organization offers menopause-specific support.

Respondents said some broader benefits, like flexible scheduling, remote options, and confidential telehealth or virtual care, would also be helpful for menopause.

Terry, Wied and Levin said they had to meet with multiple doctors before finding one who'd take their concerns seriously and who'd prescribe them hormone replacement therapy.

Levin and Wied each have a daughter. They hope better support and women's healthcare are available for their daughters when they begin menopause.

"I'm hoping that by the time my daughter goes through this, we'll have a lot more information, a lot more resources, because as it stands, it makes you super angry," Wied says.

"It's a cost on my energy, it's a cost on my stress levels, it's a cost on my bank account," Levin adds. "I don't want my daughter to have to face any of that."

As for Terry, she says she was part of a study examining potential mental health impacts of hormonal fluctuations in perimenopausal women; she hopes the research can help future generations of women. Meanwhile, her switch to independent consulting has offered her greater flexibility and a more manageable workload. But it's not perfect.

"I'm worried about retirement, I'm worried about having enough money, I'm worried about being able to continue healthcare," she says. "But I also feel like I have to take the time to feel like I'm on solid ground again."

#2882. There’s a blueprint for beating the Vikings. Can the Packers execute it?

The Minnesota Vikings have built a truly special defense. They're extremely disruptive and not particularly interested in letting opposing quarterbacks get comfortable. Mastermind DC Brian Flores has taken the concept of an aggressive defense to a new level, blitzing on 75.3% of defensive plays this season. The idea is to create confusion, force errors, and keep down-and-distances unfavorable for offenses. And to date, it's working.

So why hasn't anyone done this before? Well, because with aggression comes opportunities. There will be some opportunities on Sunday. Can Green Bay's offense capitalize? I think so. Here's how:

To make Minnesota pay for sending extra defenders, Jordan Love needs to get the ball out of his hands quickly. While Love has a reputation this year for the long-developing big play, he actually has the shortest time to throw in the NFL this season (2.60 seconds). If you watched Buffalo's win over the Vikings on Monday Night Football, you'll have noticed that Josh Allen threw 16 passes within 10 yards of the line of scrimmage. Of those, 15 were completions, and the Bills gutted out a 23-6 win.

The Packers also need the Vikings to at least somewhat respect its running game. That's easier said than done, though the Packers did finally break 100 cumulative rushing yards against the Patriots last week. While Minnesota's defense is bordering on generational, they rank 18th in rushing yards allowed. They've surrendered at least 134 rushing yards in each of their last three games. Even modest gains on early downs can help avoid the long-yardage situations where Flores' is most aggressive.

Most importantly, the Packers need to win the turnover battle. Their Week 1 loss to Minnesota was actually extremely competitive for three quarters before a late strip-sack and interception helped the Vikings pull away (I will not complain about refs, I will not complain about refs, I will not com…). While not great, Green Bay's offensive line is much better now than it was then, particularly at pass blocking. And hopefully playing at Lambeau should make communication much, much easier. That dang horn, man.

I haven't even mentioned Cousins and the Vikings offense, because I do think the real key to this game is Jordan Love and company overcoming Brian Flores. The Packers don't need to reinvent the wheel. Their offense works. They just need to stay balanced and focused, and recognize the pressure so they can turn it against the Vikings.

The Vikings are thriving on chaosNo defensive coordinator has ever blitzed as much as Brian Flores, and he'll blitz Jordan Love with even greater frequency on Sunday. Here are the keys for the Packers.

Packers' offense vs. Vikings' defense: What's different from Week 1?"It's never easy going against this defense," QB Jordan Love says

Packers slot CB Javon Bullard plans to play through injury vs. VikingsCornerback Javon Bullard missed practice on Wednesday and Thursday due to knee and ankle injuries, but said he's planning to play on Sunday.

Matt LaFleur praises Jordan Love as MVP-worthyLove's success this year goes beyond raw talent. His head coach explains why.

#2881. Rockets Marcus Smart suspended three games by NBA

Marcus Smart of the Houston Rockets was suspended yesterday for three games without pay for throwing a punch at Charles Bassey in Wednesday night's victory over the the Golden State Warriors. The incident happened in the third quarter of the Rockets' win, and Smart was ejected and given a flagrant 2.

Smart and Bassey begun arguing after an Amen Thompson drive, and then a Smart right hand connected with Bassey. Houston's head coach Ime Udoka blamed Bassey for running his mouth, however, Bassey claims it was Smart who initiated the incident by "playing dirty the whole game."

"I remember the first half, I was trying to set a screen. He grabbed my knee. He was playing dirty. I think I was trying to get a rebound. He was grabbing my hand, and I was trying to run back on defense. Then, he got up on me and just swung. Obviously, that was weak. I just think that was, honestly, fake toughness.

"It didn't surprise me. I knew he was going to do something. I don't think he was trying to play. I think he was trying to get himself out of the game. I think he was trying to chill. He wasn't trying to hoop. That was fake toughness."

Smart will miss tonight's game against the Utah Jazz, Sunday's game against the Washington Wizards, and Monday's game against the Miami Heat. Reed Sheppard likely picks up extra minutes in Smart's place. He responded on Wednesday with the some clutch play down the stretch. Let's see if that continues.

Smart is averaging 8.3 points, 2.6 rebounds, 2 assists and 1 steal per game for the Rockets while shooting 43.9 percent from the field and 40 percent from deep. Houston is currently at 9-3 and in third place in the Western Conference.

The Rockets will miss Smart, who has somewhat taken on the old Dillon Brooks agitator role, but if any team has the depth to suck it up, it's the Rockets.

#2880. ‘We will sue them’: Canada threatens Stellantis as Trump trade war reshapes Jeep production plans

Canada has threatened to sue auto giant Stellantis over the planned sale of an idled assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario, alleging breaches of a federal funding agreement after the carmaker announced plans to shift production to the U.S. amid President Donald Trump's trade war.

Speaking at a news conference on Thursday, Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said the government had served Stellantis with a formal notice of default, giving the company 30 days to address its concerns or face legal action.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters in Toronto.

Joly said Stellantis had violated its contractual commitments to the Canadian government and the workers at Brampton by announcing an agreement to sell the plant to armored vehicle manufacturer Roshel earlier in the week. She added that the company was now "on the hook" for hundreds of millions of dollars over the move.

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described the situation at the Brampton plant as "unacceptable" and a "betrayal" of its workers.

The standoff comes as the U.S. and Canada remain locked in a bitter trade dispute, with Trump repeatedly putting pressure on automakers to move vehicle production south of the border.

Stellantis, which owns auto brands including Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, had scrapped plans to build its Jeep Compass at the Brampton plant and shifted future production to a factory in Illinois amid U.S. auto tariffs and other market pressures.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said in a statement shared with CNBC that the company would return $24 million it received in government support for the Brampton Assembly's original retooling plan. This amount, however, falls significantly short of the Canadian government's own estimate of what the company owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said.

"As Canada's largest automotive employer, this decision also allows Stellantis to focus its resources on sustaining competitive manufacturing operations in Canada in the face of challenging market realities," he added.

Stellantis said Monday that it had agreed to sell the Brampton assembly plant to Roshel for an undisclosed amount.

It also acknowledged its obligations to employees at the facility and said it remains prepared to work with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union, to negotiate a settlement to assist union-represented employees.

Stellantis stock has fallen sharply since the start of the year, with its Milan-listed shares down more than 60% for 2026 so far.

— CNBC's Michael Wayland contributed to this report.

#2879. How Antoine Dupont emerged from the chaos to inspire France to epic win over South Africa

It had to be him. In the last moments of a chaotic, card-ridden contest in Paris, Antoine Dupont – France's captain fantastique – burst out of the bodies and onto the ball, one final telling intervention on a night full of them. After Louis Bielle-Biarrey's thunderous tackle had rattled a shaken Springboks, there was Dupont to pick up the pieces to cling on for a remarkable 21-19 victory.

Antoine Dupont came up with the ball at the last as France clung on in Paris (Getty)

The front-row loving French have a saying: "No scrum, no win". It was an adage proved wrong in an epic toppling of the world champions. The hosts shipped nine penalties at the set-piece alone and saw replacement tighthead Regis Montagne sent to the sin bin for persistent collapsing offences. At one stage, it looked like Sipili Falatea may follow him as Ox Nche, Malcolm Marx and Wilco Louw repeatedly drove back and dismantled their overmatched French counterparts. Somehow he, and France, survived.

For so long, there were agonising echoes of the 2023 World Cup quarter-final for the hosts as the Springboks threatened a late heist. When Marx ploughed over at the back of a maul juggernaut with nine minutes to play to bring South Africa within two, they looked the favourites. But Manie Libbok – not for the first time – was errant from the tee. One last assault was launched, steady progress made. But then came Bielle-Biarrey, then Dupont. Game over.

Maxime Lucu and Antoine Dupont steered France to victory (Reuters)

The French captain had been a figure of outsized focus in this meeting of Nations Championship heavyweights. Injuries to Fabien Galthie's first four fly-half choices left a vacancy, with le petit general ostensibly the man elected to fill it. He wore No 10, but played one door over; Maxime Lucu, his playmaking partner, instead took on the traditional fly half duties.

South Africa engaged in their own tomfoolery, too. Little more than an hour before kick off came news of a scrum-time switcheroo: Nche, Marx and Louw swapped on to the bench; Gerhard Steenekamp, Jan-Hendrik Wessels and Thomas du Toit starting instead. The trio wore their substitutes' shirts in a sign of the confusion – such a late switch would not be permitted at a World Cup or in the Six Nations, but was within regulation under Nations Championship rules.

Perhaps it riled a French side already with backs against the wall. With the help of a forward pack lended extra heft by the return of Gregory Alldritt, Dupont and Lucu combined to unsettle and unravel the Springboks defence, varying their play wonderfully in a first quarter where much went right.

South Africa's forwards threatened to turn the game their way (Reuters)

Aaron Grandidier-Nkanang had already twice threatened to sear into the right corner by the time Dupont and Lucu were utilised in unison to find him; it proved third time lucky for the Bromley-born wing. Lady luck was shining on Les Bleus, too, the use of Kalvin Gourgues' head inadvertent; Theo Attissogbe did not mind as he dotted down a friendly ricochet. By the time Bielle-Biarrey zoomed onto an inch-perfect grubber from Dupont, France were flying.

South Africa, oddly errant and error-prone, had struggled to find their fluency, bar one Wessels maul score. They thought they had another lineout try of a different kind when Kurt-Lee Arendse burst in on the stroke of half-time, but the television match official deemed him offside as the throw was made. Pernickety? Perhaps. France gleefully trotted down the tunnel all the same.

Soon after the restart, so did Jasper Wiese – though he wore a scowl. The No 8 has worked hard to improve the discipline that pockmarked his early career but his ruck challenge on Kalvin Gourgues was ugly, and rightly received a 20-minute red card. Having lost Lood de Jager to a sending off in this fixture 12 months ago, a 14-man Springboks would have to pull the game out of the fire again.

Louis Bielle-Biarrey's tackle on Jesse Kriel helped seal victory for France (Getty)

They so nearly managed it. First Cobus Reinach sharply sniped the ball beneath the posts after his forwards had done the heavy sledding soon after a yellow card to Grandidier-Nkanang, before Marx set up a grandstand finish. France were blown back repeatedly – both by the South African scrum, and referee Andrea Piardi's whistle. It was a night for titans: Pieter-Steph du Toit did not deserve to be on the losing side, but it was Dupont, of course, who made sure he had the last word.
