#2877. CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations 2026: How the 32 franchises stack up

For the first time, hockey team valuations have crossed the $4 billion threshold, with CNBC's NHL valuations led by the Toronto Maple Leafs, at $5.4 billion, the New York Rangers, at $4.7 billion, and the Montreal Canadiens, at $4.1 billion. The average NHL team is now worth $2.71 billion, 23% more than a year ago.

Don't be fooled by the lone NHL control sale to close over the past 12 months: the $1.8 billion sale of the Pittsburgh Penguins to the Hoffmann family. Although the transaction was approved by the league in June, the price was negotiated about 18 months ago.

Look instead at the latest minority stake transactions. In March the NHL approved the sale of a 12.5% stake in the Carolina Hurricanes by Tom Dundon in a transaction that valued the team at $2.66 billion. Scott Malkin and other shareholders are close to selling a 15% stake in the New York Islanders at a $3 billion valuation. Sports bankers tell CNBC these deals are representative of what control stakes in the two teams would sell for given the hot market for sports teams.

"Like the other major sports leagues there is a strong demand to own hockey teams because their media content values are high and there is a scarcity of teams," said Sal Galatioto, whose firm, Galatioto Sports Partners, advises clients on both the buy and sell sides of sports team transactions. "Capitalism and technology are creating more wealth and the NHL, like the NFL, NBA and MLB, is an industry with a very long product life cycle, a hedge against risk in AI."

The NHL's current national media rights deals with Walt Disney and Warner Bros. Discovery are worth a combined annual average of close to $650 million through the 2027-28 season, but given the league's strong television ratings last season, a big increase could be in the offing.

"The 2025-26 NHL regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from last year and the highest average in more than a decade," Sports Media Watch wrote. "The 25% increase is on the high end of a general rise in sports viewership that includes gains of 35% for the NBA, 19% for men's college basketball and 10% for the NFL."

Sports Media Watch also wrote that the 2026 NHL Stanley Cup Playoffs averaged 1.8 million viewers in the U.S. across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports. That's up 68% from 2025, and it's the highest average on record for the playoffs on American television, going back to 1994, it said.

The NHL is pondering adding teams in cities such as Houston and Atlanta, and another factor fueling NHL valuations is the belief that the expansion fees paid by the new teams would surpass $2 billion. The Seattle Kraken, the NHL's most recent expansion team, paid a $650 million fee to join the league for the 2021-22 season.

According to CNBC's figures, revenue for the league's 32 teams averaged $257 million for the 2025-26 season, 6% more than the previous year, while EBITDA — earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization — averaged $48 million per team, a decline of 11% due to arena expenses. The league's profitability should rebound as money spent on hospitality, concessions and merchandise stores boosts revenue.

In April 2025 the NHL and Rogers Communications agreed to a 12-year national Canadian media rights deal worth $7.79 billion, based on exchange rates as of Nov. 24, that will begin with the 2026-27 season. That's more than twice the amount the league is getting from its existing 12-year deal with Rogers. This will help push up the league's hockey-related revenue — which includes media rights, sponsorships, premium seating, tickets, concessions and parking — to around $7.25 billion for the 2026-27 season, 7% more than last season.

CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations are current enterprise values — equity plus net debt — calculated using revenue multiples, and include the economics of the team's arena, including non-NHL revenue that accrues to the team's owner. The valuations are meant to represent what a control stake valuation would go for in an arm's-length transaction and do not include the value of the arena or the real estate it sits on.

Revenue figures are net of revenue sharing and the 35% of home playoff non-premium gate revenue that goes toward the league's revenue-sharing pool. Revenue multiples are based on historical transaction prices, as well as what investors, league executives and bankers say about the current market.

CNBC values exclude other businesses related to the teams with separate financial statements, such as the Edmonton Oilers' mixed-use real estate project. For teams that sell their own merchandise, such as the Oilers, CNBC deducts the cost of goods sold from merchandise sales. Likewise, CNBC excludes the market value of affiliates/equity method investments to ensure consistent enterprise value-to-revenue and enterprise value-to-EBITDA multiples. Debt figures are the latest available and include both team debt and stadium debt.

CNBC incorporates the value of a pending new arena in our valuations if construction has begun and financing has been secured. For example, the $2.25 billion valuation for the Calgary Flames incorporates their new arena, which is scheduled to open in time for the 2027-28 season. Likewise, CNBC valuations anticipate a substantial increase in the NHL's next U.S. national broadcasting deal, which would begin with the 2028-29 season.

Revenue and EBITDA figures are for the 2025-26 season and net of revenue sharing and player escrow. CNBC presents revenue and operating income figures on a cash basis, rather than an accrual basis. CNBC figures are different from the league's official hockey-related revenue because we include non-NHL arena revenue that goes to the teams. When the owner of the arena also owns both the NHL team and NBA team that play in the same building — as is the case for several teams, including Toronto's Maple Leafs and Raptors, who are both owned by Rogers Communications and play in Scotiabank Arena — we divide revenue from non-sports events held at the arena evenly between the two teams.

All valuation figures are in U.S. dollars. The exchange rate for revenue and EBITDA is based on a one-year average, from June 2025 to June 2026, of CA$1 = US$0.72, and the exchange rate for team values and debt, as of Sept. 21, is CA$1 = US$0.71.

Sources for CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations include team owners, investors and executives, as well as sports bankers and league consultants; public documents such as stadium lease agreements, stadium authority budget audits and credit rating reports; and sponsorship and broadcasting industry executives. Certain figures were provided by league executives who spoke to CNBC on the condition of anonymity because agreements are private and they do not have permission to speak about them publicly. Figures that could not be confirmed with sources are CNBC estimates.

— CNBC's Jungwoo Ethan Kim contributed to this report.

#2874. By the Numbers: Is the Packers’ offense sustainable?

As I tend to do every week, I found myself talking about the Green Bay Packers a lot this week. Some of that was just muttering to myself in the hallway of my house, but sometimes I was talking to real, live people and sometimes those conversations were recorded. One question that keeps coming up has been this: is the Packers offense sustainable?

On its face, the answer is "probably not." We're coming off a performance where Jordan Love had fire shooting from his hands: completing 79.3% of his passes for 372 yards and 3 TDs. He posted that 79.2% completion rate while bombing: 27.6% of his attempts were 20+ yards down the field (his highest rate of the season).

The question of sustainability revolves around Jordan Love ("can he keep this up"), but it's really more about the run game. Can an offense continue to be successful into the playoffs with a high pass rate and a bad running game?

No matter where you look, the run game metrics are bad. They're dead-last in EPA per Play at -0.26 (per NFL Pro). They're dead last in DVOA at -32.6% (per FTN). They're 31st in Yards Per Attempt at 3.0 (per Pro Football Reference). Pick your favorite football analytics site and they're going to be right near the bottom in rushing on every single one of them.

In looking at the overall team, I believe it's sustainable. On top of being an aggressive attacker down the field, Love has become a very good operator in the quick game, and the Packers have the offensive pieces to make that work. Whether that's working an old favorite like Slant/Flat or hitting some of those quick outs he has become so fond of, it's an area of his game that has seen a ton of improvement over the years. If they need to supplement the lack of a run game with a quick pass game – and take enough deep shots to keep defenses from sitting on the underneath routes – that feels like it could work. Combine that with a defense that has been playing extremely well (2nd most turnovers, 5th in DVOA, 9th in EPA per Play) and that feels like a recipe for sustainability. But is it really?

I dug into some numbers to see if that's something that we've seen before. Have we seen a team this bad at running the ball find success? I had to define success, so I determined that any team that made the playoffs could be assumed to have some sort of sustained success. So I grabbed every team that made the playoffs from 1999-2025 and looked at a handful of their metrics during the regular season to see if this is something that has happened. If you're asking "why 1999", the answer is because that's the furthest back the data I am using for this goes back.

The main metrics I looked at were Pass Rate, EPA and Success Rate.

Here's where the Packers sit. For these numbers, I'm only looking at games after Week 3, since the offensive line was non-functional for the first few weeks. Is that cheating? Maybe! But it felt like the best thing to do, and I'm making the rules here.

We're going to lock that Packers line so we can keep it in mind, then look at some other teams that may fit the bill. If there is a * next to a team name, it means that team was in the Super Bowl that season.

These are the highest pass rate teams in our group. As you can see, a pass rate of 61.1% isn't crazy given some of these other teams. It's still relatively high – a pass rate of 61.1% would rank 97th highest out of 336 teams – but it's not some crazy outlier. Among these teams, the Packers' EPA per Pass far exceeds the other teams, but their running game is worse than all of them except for the 2022 Bucs. Their Passing Success is better than everyone except the 2021 Bucs and the 2011 Lions, but their running game is worse than all of them in terms of success.

That led me to look at Rush Success Rate. Where does the rushing success rate from this year's Packers team stack up against our sample?

The Packers would still be a bit of an outlier – their 36.3% rush success would be the 22nd worst success rate among our 336 teams – but it has been done. The one that jumps out (because of course it does) is the 2007 Packers. I'm not alone in believing that, if they hadn't lost to the Giants, they would have won the Super Bowl that year. For this year's Packers to be significantly better in EPA per Pass – and slightly better in Rush Success Rate – is pretty surprising. That team also had a slightly higher Pass Rate than this year's Packers, as well. That 2007 team had a better Pass Success Rate, so they were better at moving the chains, but this team is better at generating points. If you want to point to a volatility point, something like that would be it. A high EPA per Play but low Success Rate could suggest that a team is punching a bit above their weight.

We could do this all day, but we'll end with this. I wanted to see what teams had a Rushing EPA worse than this year's Packers:

Those are all the teams with a worse Rush EPA than this year's Packers. A few of those teams (2022 Bucs, 1999 Lions, 2014 Colts) had a higher pass rate, but most of them were more run-heavy than this year's Packers, despite having worse results while doing so.

Of these teams, the Packers outpaced all of them in EPA per Pass and Pass Success Rate. They have a better overall Success Rate than all but 2 of these teams (2022 Bucs, 2014 Colts).

We'll stop here, or we'll be here all day. There's more that can be combed through, but I think we have a pretty good idea of where this Packers team stands. Let's close by revisiting our question:

Question: Is this Packers offense sustainable?

Answer: History tells us that it is, but it's also a fairly narrow path to walk. Teams with this makeup have been "sustainable" enough to make the playoffs numerous times, but it's not the norm.

Albums listened to: Blondshell – Violins; Leem of Earth – See; Remi Wolf – Mud

#2873. U.S. seizes $1.2 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance

U.S. authorities have seized around $1.2 billion in virtual assets stolen from cryptocurrency exchange Binance last month, the Department of Justice said Thursday, as it vowed action to tackle cyberscams originating from North Korea.

Over $6 billion was stolen from industry giant Binance on Oct. 29, which the DOJ attributed to actors linked to the North Korean military.

Collective efforts between the U.S. government and private sector have resulted in more than $2.7 billion of that amount being frozen.

The FBI blames the attack, which shook confidence in crypto market security, on members of the North Korean military it refers to as "TraderTraitor."

The DOJ said it was taking coordinated action against what it called the North Korean government's "schemes to fund its regime through the massive theft of cryptocurrency."

That includes "the court-authorized seizure and restraint of more than $1.2 billion worth of cryptocurrency recently stolen from Binance," it said.

DOJ units, law enforcement partners and virtual asset service providers have been working to intercept the stolen assets.

But efforts to track and freeze the assets are ongoing, as TraderTraitor actors continue to launder them through various cryptocurrency channels.

Authorities have also seized cloud-based infrastructure used in a massive identity impersonation scam known as "FaceStealer," which has seen more than $400 million worth of cryptocurrency stolen over 18 months, the DOJ said.

The scheme sees hackers set up fake video meetings using stolen accounts and recordings of people known to the target by using AI-generated deepfakes. The hackers then say a technical problem requires a fix, install malware on a computer and take over the victim's communications, steal their cryptocurrency and target their contacts — sometimes months or more than a year after the initial compromise.

Individuals working in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries are prime targets.

Assistant Director in Charge Patrick Grandy, of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office, called FaceStealer "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user."

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships. If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough," Grandy added.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor said in a statement.

"America will fight back against that corrupt regime's effort to profit through cybercrime. The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

#2872. Ugly wins count, too. And the Suns will take every one.

You know what I like? When the Phoenix Suns win basketball games. Sometimes it's not pretty, and sometimes the journey to the final buzzer is far more stressful than it needs to be. But as long as the Suns walk away with a victory, there's reason to feel positive. Following Thursday night's win over the Charlotte Hornets, that's exactly how I'm feeling.

Phoenix has now won two consecutive games and sits one victory shy of the .500 mark. Once again, the bench unit provided the spark, serving as the catalyst that helped propel the Suns to a 17-point advantage. For a stretch, it appeared Phoenix was on its way to a relatively comfortable victory, the kind where you could sit back, relax, and enjoy the basketball without wondering how the team might find a way to complicate things.

But this is the Phoenix Suns we're talking about. That 17-point lead? Evaporated.

The Suns were outrebounded 52-31, continuing a troubling trend that has plagued them throughout the early portion of the season. And quite honestly, had Charlotte been even remotely competent at taking care of the basketball, Phoenix might have messed around and lost this one. The Hornets committed a staggering 27 turnovers, and while it's tempting to look at that number and credit the Suns' defensive pressure, the reality is a little more complicated. Phoenix finished with 11 steals, certainly demonstrating its ability to disrupt passing lanes and create opportunities in transition. But a significant portion of Charlotte's turnovers came from careless passes and poor decision-making, the kind of mistakes that reveal a team struggling with connectivity and offensive cohesion.

Still, Phoenix deserves credit for capitalizing on those mistakes. The Suns generated 38 points off turnovers, a massive contribution that ultimately played a significant role in securing the victory. They also took care of the basketball themselves, committing only eight turnovers, which provided a welcome example of offensive discipline.

There were other positives as well. Despite being thoroughly dominated on the glass, Phoenix matched Charlotte with 38 points in the paint. And while the Hornets held a 16-7 advantage in second-chance points, the damage could have been considerably worse given the disparity in rebounding. That doesn't excuse the ongoing problems on the boards, but it does provide some perspective on how the Suns managed to survive another evening in which securing missed shots appeared to be an optional activity.

The rebounding remains a major concern, and there are certainly other issues worth examining over the next couple of days before the Suns welcome the Los Angeles Lakers to town on Sunday. We'll have plenty of time to dissect what went wrong, what needs to improve, and why this team continues to make certain aspects of the game far more difficult than necessary.

But there were encouraging moments, too. The bench continues to produce, the Suns are generating points off turnovers, and they're beginning to string together victories despite their imperfections. And ultimately, the most important statistic is the one that matters more than anything else. The Phoenix Suns won the basketball game. I'll take it.

Goodie sparked the offense against the Clippers, and his 18 points in 13 minutes made him the easiest Bright Side Baller selection of the season.

#2871. Christian McCaffrey says Kaelon Black makes RB room ‘really special’

Christian McCaffrey sees the potential for something exciting in the San Francisco 49ers' running back room, and rookie Kaelon Black is a big reason why.

McCaffrey praised Black's ability to provide a different level to the running game when he plays, highlighting how the two backs can complement each other by rotating throughout the game and keeping each other fresh.

"When KB comes in, it's such a spark," McCaffrey said, via The Athletic's Vic Tafur. "When I can come in and come out and get my wind, he goes in and balls out, comes out, gets his wind. If we can just continue to do that, man, I think we can be really special."

Black, a third-round pick out of Indiana, won the backup running back job during the offseason and has steadily carved out a role behind McCaffrey through the 49ers' first eight games.

The rookie delivered one of his best performances Sunday against the Las Vegas Raiders, rushing 11 times for 70 yards. McCaffrey added 68 rushing yards, a touchdown, two catches for 19 yards and another score in the 38-17 victory. The two combined for 138 rushing yards and 157 yards from scrimmage, giving San Francisco a productive one-two punch in the backfield.

McCaffrey has remained one of the 49ers' most important offensive weapons, but finding ways to manage his workload is important given the physical demands of his position. Black's emergence gives Kyle Shanahan another capable option who can keep the ground game productive when McCaffrey needs a breather.

If the two continue building on that chemistry, McCaffrey believes the 49ers could have something special in their backfield.

#2870. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2869. Jensen Huang scandal casts doubt on Nvidia’s AI dominance — how we got here

Nvidia is facing one the biggest crises in its 33 year history, with founder Jensen Huang resigning as CEO Thursday after multiple reported affairs, including one with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy. Now CFO Colette Kress has taken the helm as Nvidia's interim CEO.

The implications of Huang's affair with Alice Huang run far deeper than infidelity. That's because her ties to Nvidia customer Megaspeed put her in the middle of a sprawling effort to accumulate AI chips across Southeast Asia. And Megaspeed is under investigation for possibly diverting restricted Nvidia chips into the hands of Chinese companies.

The relationship between Jensen Huang and Alice Huang dates back to 2023, according to The New York Times. That was a "turbulent" period for Nvidia, Jensen Huang told CNBC during an interview at the time. Nvidia was in the midst of redesigning some chips to comply with the first in a series of strict export controls imposed by the U.S. government that restricted sales to China.

"The regulation is going to be just fine. We're going to be able to work through it," Huang told CNBC from Nvidia's headquarters in February 2023. The interview was for a documentary about how Huang created Nvidia and built it into the backbone of the AI boom.

That same day, CNBC also interviewed Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, with whom Jensen Huang reportedly had an affair.

It's not the first time Nvidia has faced an uncertain future. The company nearly went bankrupt in the early years after it was founded in 1993.

"Every company makes mistakes and I make a lot of them," Huang said in the 2023 interview. "And some of them put the company in peril."

In the early months of the AI boom, after OpenAI launched ChatGPT in November 2022, Nvidia was on the precipice of historic growth. Jensen Huang told CNBC he'd like to keep running the company for "three or four decades," as long as "I feel that I'm making a real contribution to the company."

#2868. James Harden admits to being ‘James Harden’

Thirty-two points on 14 shots in a much-needed Cleveland Cavaliers win over the New York Knicks was a good reminder of who James Harden is. And in case there was still any confusion, the former MVP made sure to clear everything up afterward.

"My name is James Harden. I used to score a lot of points," Harden told reporters Wednesday evening.

That clarification was needed based on how Harden's first 11 outings had gone. He entered Wednesday's game in New York averaging an uncharacteristic 15.3 points per game, shooting a dreadful 36.4% from the field. Harden has been a step slow and unable to create space off the dribble.

There's a downstream effect to Harden not being able to score. The passing lanes dry up when defenders don't have to help cut off his drives. This has left him as an inefficient scorer and inept playmaker at the start of the season.

Wednesday, Harden showed that he still has the talent to be a dynamic scoring guard. He got the outside shot working in the second quarter, and then expanded his game from there. Defenders had to overplay him on the perimeter, which allowed him to attack inside. He used that to his advantage to score nine points in the fourth quarter, which included two finishes inside the paint.

In short, he was the Harden we've seen wreck defenses for over a decade and a half, not the one that we saw in the first 11 games this season.

Anything bought from the links helps support Fear the Sword. You can buy the comfortable Cavs hoodie HERE. You can also shop all of Homage's Cavs gear HERE.

The Cavs would be a championship contender if they could guarantee performances like this from Harden in the playoffs.

Being able to score and playmake at this level would mean teams couldn't load up to stop Donovan Mitchell, and it would create easy scoring opportunities for Jarrett Allen and Evan Mobley inside. But you can't ensure this version of Harden on a nightly basis, or even for big games he gets up for. Age catches up to every player, especially a 37-year-old like Harden who has played nearly 50,000 minutes in his 18-year career.

Instead of counting on Harden to play as he did in his prime, he's trying to find other ways to adapt his game to his current situation.

"I just focus on myself, things I need to do, and try to get in really good shape and learn my teammates and learn how to play a different brand of basketball, because I'm not the same fast person that I was like five or six years ago," Harden said.

Harden is still playing his way into shape, admitting that he wasn't as young as he used to be, so he "can't just be ready" at the start of the season. It takes a "bit of time to get adjusted to the speed, the pace, all of the above." But even when he does get back up to speed, the explosiveness isn't there for him to be the guy he was with the Houston Rockets.

Instead, Harden will need to find different ways to impact winning in this new reality he finds himself in. If someone can make that adjustment, it's a player as skilled and as smart as Harden. That adjustment won't happen overnight. There will undoubtedly be more lows like we saw in the embarrassing loss to the Charlotte Hornets earlier this week, but the Cavs are more than willing to endure those games if it means Harden can eventually find his way. Because in the end, the Cavaliers' hopes and dreams for this season and the next two depend on Harden putting the pieces together.

"Now I still score points, but I'm finding other ways to impact the game as well," Harden said. "And I think that's what people probably don't understand."

#2867. PFF grades and advanced stats from the Giants’ loss to the Commanders

The post-mortem on a game the New York Giants should have won but didn't is never fun. Let's see what we can learn from the Pro Football Focus grades and advanced stats about who played well and who didn't. Before looking at the offense and defense, let's note that PFF gave Jordan Stout his lowest grade of the season because of that 12-yard shank in the fourth quarter that gave the Commanders decent field position to start their winning drive, and of course Dominic Zvada got a low grade for his field goal miss from 32 yards out (though not as low as the first Washington and the Houston games in which he had multiple misses). That's part of the story, but there was blame to go around on the offense and defense. And let's not forget the coaching. In the old days we used to speak of a "prevent defense" that tried to hold a lead by playing soft coverage that stopped big plays. Well, last night John Harbaugh played what might be called a "prevent offense," being scared off field goal attempts and punting in Commanders' territory after Zvada'a early miss and trying to wind down the clock with running plays and quarterback keepers instead of calling easy pitch-and-catch plays to keep the ball out of Jayden Daniels' hands.

PFF did not give a single elite (80 or higher) grade to anyone on the offense last night (part of that was due to the weather conditions, Washington only had one elite-graded offensive player themselves, Ben Sinnott). J.J. McCarthy received an overall very good offense grade, more because of his running (three scrambles for 29 yards) than his passing. However, his passing grade was brought down by his final offensive play, an interception deep downfield on which he was blitzed and on which Malik Nabers did not make much of an effort to help him out. Overall, J.J. handled the pressure well, doing about as well as when he was kept clean:

J.J. was blitzed on 45% of dropbacks: 2 of his 3 turnover-worthy plays came on blitzes, but 2 of his 3 big-time throws also came on blitzes. Overall, with 293 yards, a pretty good performance.

Of note is the performance of the offensive line. Even though McCarthy was pressured on 35% of his dropbacks, the OL performed pretty well overall. Jon Runyan and Sisi Mauigoa had great pass-blocking games, Runyan having a clean stat line and Mauigoa only giving up one hurry. The run block grades weren't as good but not terrible either. Andrew Thomas and Jermaine Eluemunor both graded very good in pass coverage with 2 hurries charged to AT and one QB hit to Eluemunor. Bryan Hudson graded below average in pass blocking with 2 hurries and just barely below average in run blocking. Patrick Ricard had his best game as a Giant, especially in run blocking. Cam Skattebo fell just below the Very Good category with his 18 rushes for 78 yards; maybe he was graded down a bit for eating before the game and throwing up on the sideline 🙂

None of the receivers graded as high as Very Good, though Malachi Fields and Isaiah Likely came close. The reason Likely didn't grade higher despite his 9 of 11, 127 yard, 1 TD performance was because of his fumble; PFF grades turnovers very severely. Malik Nabers only had 44 yards on 5 catches in 7 targets, but his very low grade reflects his fumble which cost the Giants a scoring opportunity.

The defense was a different story. There was one excellent defensive performance last night – the defense was not the main reason the Giants lost the game. Ar'Darius Washington has seen his playing time increase the past three weeks, and last night he graded an outstanding 89.9 overall, 91.4 in coverage. Washington was targeted 6 times and gave up 3 completions for a total of only 11 yards and a 16.7 passer rating. He made back-to-back great plays, a pass reception stop and then an interception, with the game on the line and the Commanders in scoring position. His only blemish was 3 missed tackles, but he did have 5 tackles and 2 assists.

The rest of the secondary did not perform as well. Deonte Banks received a barely below average coverage and overall defense grade; if he turns his head and tries to play the ball on the deep pass to Treylon Burks with less than two minutes left in the game, there's no flag and the Giants may end up winning the game. Jevon Holland had the big interception, but he also gave up a TD and 40 yards total on pass plays. Dru Phillips graded 53.5 overall and 48.8 in coverage primarily because he gave up the game-losing TD to Stefon Diggs, but he also missed two tackles. Greg Newsome also graded below average, giving up 22 yards on 2 receptions in 3 targets and missing a tackle. Jason Pinnock graded low as well before leaving the game; he missed 2 tackles and graded low in coverage, primarily because he was covering on the Daniels TD pass to Ben Sinnott.

The Giants' most high profile defensive players (with Brian Burns out for the season) are now Abdul Carter and Arvell Reese. Both were graded Very Good overall by PFF. Reese did nothing in the pass rush last night, but he did have 7 tackles and 5 assists, three of them stops, with no missed tackles. He also gave up four completions in four targets in pass coverage, but they only went for 21 yards total with only 9 YAC, warranting a Very Good coverage grade. Carter is the more difficult evaluation. He continues to be unable to get to the QB for sacks, but he did have a QB hit and 2 hurries plus 2 tackles and an assist, resulting in a Very Good overall defense grade. Micah McFadden had a QB hurry and 9 tackles plus an assist, but he only graded average overall because he surrendered 4 catches and 55 yards on pass defense.

The rest of the defensive line was a bunch of mediocre to poor performances. Kayvon Thibodeaux only had one QB hurry and graded fairly low on run defense despite 7 tackles and an assist, suggesting that he was just blocked out of some running plays. D.J. Reader had his worst game as a Giant, with only 1 hurry and 1 tackle. Shelby Harris got his first sack and had a hurry and 2 tackles, 2 assists. Bobby Jamison-Travis continues to get snaps but had only a tackle and an assist, but that was better than Darius Alexander, who only had one tackle.

The Giants' defense overall can be summed up as: Not bad, but not great either. Here is an overall ranking of NFL defenses in a number of categories:

Overall, the Giants rank No. 12 in the NFL in points per game given up. That sounds pretty good, although you would have to say that other than the Rams, they haven't played any of the NFL's powerhouse offenses. They are below average in sacks, slightly below average in takeaways, and among the league's worst in tackles for loss. It could be a lot worse, but they don't scare anyone either. Speaking of scaring, note that the Jaguars, next week's opponent, are No. 2 in the league in points per game allowed despite not scoring high in any individual category, unlike the No. 1 Vikings, who rank highest in sacks. Somehow the Jags are keeping teams out of the end zone in most of their games. They have the second best red zone D in the NFL, while the Giants' red zone D is near the bottom:

That matters, because as I have noted, John Harbaugh and his offensive coordinator Matt Nagy have mostly been very conservative in their play calling and fourth down decisions. That's quantified below by Kevin Cole of Unexpected Points:

According to the plot above, the Giants go for it on 4th down only about 1 in 3 times when the situation indicates at least a one point win probability added by going for it, one of the lowest rates in the NFL. Here's a related stat about the same philosophy:

At this time it seems appropriate to remind ourselves of the wisdom of Herm Edwards (even though he said this in a different context):

Related to this, here are the NFL rankings for the three-and-out offense rates just over the last three weeks, conveniently coinciding with the J.J. McCarthy as Giants starter era:

Perhaps related to this, here are the checkdown rates for NFL QBs for the past 4 weeks:

So is McCarthy a gunslinger who eschews taking the easy few yards in favor of taking a shot downfield? That might be one way to interpret the deep pass to Nabers that was intercepted on the Giants' last drive, but the question is whether the Nagy offense is giving him an easy answer underneath and J.J. is ignoring or not seeing it vs. giving him no answer at all. On the Nabers INT the pressure up the middle came quickly, but Malachi Fields was right there for McCarthy to throw shallow to and pick up a few yards with plenty of time left for a few more plays:

All that said, in a matchup of one of the league's most highly regarded young QBs against a QB drafted 8 spots lower and jettisoned for a 5th round pick, the performances of Jayden Daniels and J.J. McCarthy weren't all that different last night, and where they were, the difference mostly favored McCarthy:

Daniels had a higher completion rate (69.7% to 63.9%), but J.J.'s CPOE was near zero while Daniels' was -2.1%. McCarthy's ddep throw rate was 11.1% vs. Daniels' 3.0%. The two had almost identical times to throw (2.59 vs. 2.55 s), but McCarthy was pressured 38.5% of the time to Daniels' 20.5%. McCarthy threw into tight windows 11.1% of the time vs. Daniels' 6.1%.

The bottom line is that no one questions Jayden Daniels' ability and status as one of the NFL's best young QBs. McCarthy has traveled a more difficult road. To what extent that is his own fault vs. that of the team that drafted him is a matter for discussion and difficult to ascertain from the outside. But nothing he has shown as a Giant suggests that he cannot be a productive QB at the NFL level. It didn't work out for him last night, but he gave the Giants a chance to win.

#2865. Trump says he wants to ‘get Cuba done’ before leaving office, would consider military force

U.S. President Donald Trump has said he wants to "get Cuba done" before leaving office in 2029, adding that he would "certainly consider" military intervention against the communist-run Caribbean island.

In an interview with conservative radio host Hugh Hewitt on Thursday, Trump said his administration was monitoring the situation in Havana "very closely" and described Cuba as a "failed" state.

"I want to get Cuba done before I leave," Trump said. His comments marked the first time he has put a timeline on possible military intervention to topple Cuban authorities, according to Politico.

Trump said Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whose parents are from Cuba, "would love to do it" and said he would "certainly consider" a short military operation.

It comes as the U.S. continues to enforce a comprehensive economic embargo against Cuba, a campaign which has contributed to a deepening energy and economic crisis on the island. Cuba has suffered repeated nationwide blackouts in recent months, as well as fuel and food shortages.

The Cuban Embassy in Washington, D.C., estimated on Thursday that the cost of the U.S. blockade to Cuba between March 2025 and February 2026 amounted to $8 billion.

Earlier this month, the Treasury Department imposed further sanctions on Cuban oil tankers and shipping companies and threatened to target any international entity or person doing business within its marine or maritime industries as part of a push "to end the Cuban regime's malign activities, both in Cuba and across our hemisphere."

In response, Cuba's Embassy in Washington, D.C., said these steps "deepen an energy siege that already leaves hospitals without reliable power and families facing blackouts. The blockade is not targeted at a government. It is collective punishment that hits ordinary people every day."

Trump has previously talked up the prospect of a "friendly takeover" of Havana and said the White House could turn its sights on Cuba after the Iran war. The U.S. president has also said he could do anything he wanted with the country, adding that he thinks he will have the "honor" of "taking Cuba."

In May, Cuban Energy Minister Vicente de la O Levy said the country of roughly 10 million had run out of fuel oil and diesel, describing the country's predicament as "extremely tense."

Analysts have typically been skeptical about the prospect of a U.S. military campaign against Cuba, suggesting that further economic pressure against the island's authorities appears more likely.

#2862. Apple, Alphabet and Uber survived major founder transitions. Nvidia will be a harder test

Jensen Huang is not the first CEO to leave a company he helped build.

But experts told CNBC that his sudden resignation from Nvidia at a critical juncture is unique and leaves his eventual successor with challenges in leading the company that's powering the artificial intelligence boom.

In 33 years, Huang, who resigned on Thursday amid reports of extramarital affairs, including with a woman U.S. intelligence believes is a spy for China, made the company he co-founded the world's most valuable.

Former Goldman Sachs CEO Lloyd Blankfein wrote on X that he was "indispensable" but "the graveyards are filled with indispensable men," referencing a quote attributed to French leader Charles de Gaulle.

While the board looks for a new CEO and CFO Colette Kress does the job temporarily; analysts say Huang's charisma and bullish drive will be hard to replicate.

"I'm not sure we've seen a situation quite like this, where the founder and CEO of one of the world's largest companies faces such a sudden departure amid scandal," Michael Withers, professor of management at the University of Notre Dame's Mendoza College of Business, told CNBC.

Whoever follows will "face challenges that go well beyond those of a typical CEO replacing a founder," he added.

Apple's image was inseparable from Steve Jobs when he resigned in 2011. But unlike Nvidia, the company had had other CEOs — and an internal successor.

Tim Cook invested in Apple's chip strategy and services business and became a key corporate diplomat in dealings with the U.S. and Chinese governments. He was known as an operator who controlled Apple's supply chain.

Cook had "considerable preparation" for becoming CEO, Withers said. Apple's market cap went from about $350 billion to $4 trillion in his 15-year tenure.

Larry Page and Sergey Brin left their executive roles at Alphabet in 2019, 21 years after founding Google.

Page and Brin's departures were expected. Page said at the time that it was the "natural time" to simplify the company's management structure. Alphabet shares are up more than 300% since then under Sunder Pichai.

Travis Kalanick left Uber in 2017 after mounting pressure over a series of scandals and governance concerns.

But his departure "unfolded over several months, and while it certainly attracted attention, it pales in comparison to what Nvidia is currently facing," Withers added.

Former Expedia CEO Dara Khosrowshahi repaired the organization and found a path to profitability.

Withers said successor CEOs often struggle "under normal circumstances" to juggle maintaining what made a company successful while establishing their own leadership.

"It becomes considerably more difficult when the transition follows such a high-profile scandal involving the company's founder," he added.

Huang had an extraordinary 60 direct reports, as part of a flat structure that favored fast communication over layered hierarchies.

Withers told CNBC Nvidia did not appear to have a succession plan for Huang, who is also often described as inextricably linked to the company.

"We're talking about a company that was, until recently, the largest in the world by market capitalization. That's a pretty remarkable situation," he added.

As well as having to "step out of his shadow," the new CEO will have to deal with the "scandal" arising from Huang's conduct and "the extent to which it sticks with Nvidia," Withers said.

Huang's successor will also have to tackle the longer-term business strategy, while picking up the relationships he managed.

Huang cultivated a close relationship with U.S. President Donald Trump and accompanied him to China in May. Huang consistently pushed for the U.S. to relax export restrictions that prevent Nvidia from selling its chips to the world's second-largest economy.

He also managed close relationships with the bosses of the company's key suppliers, from SK Hynix to Samsung.

Storm Uru, co-head of the Global Innovation team at Nvidia investor Liontrust Asset Management, said two key priorities were changing company culture and managing the supply chain.

The supply chain was "where Jensen was able to provide a vision for the future and able to bring people to the table and to expand capacity to enable the build-out of this technology," Uru told CNBC, adding it mattered especially "when supply chains are already so tight."

Nvidia continues to face rising competition from rivals such as AMD and hyperscalers, which are ramping up efforts to develop their own chips.

Investors noted that the new CEO will have a roadmap of products for the next few years, unaffected by Huang's removal.

"We have to look beyond Jensen ... there are thousands upon thousands of engineers," Joanne Feeney, partner and portfolio manager, Advisors Capital Management, told CNBC on Thursday, as pressure was mounting on Huang to go.

#2860. Preview: Jazz looking to take liftoff in NBA Cup Game

Here we are again. We here at SLC Dunk can whisper to you sweet dreams that each game is an encouraging sign that the team is trending in the right direction, but the harsh reality is the Jazz are 1-11. Games are more about keeping your dignity and pride as an organisation, steering clear of the team's worst start in franchise history (1-17), when you're trying to win games. The musty odor the Jazz emit with each fourth-quarter meltdown escalates.

Hopes of a late-blooming win streak were swatted yet again on Wednesday, when the middling Orlando Magic forced a 21-5 run at the Delta Center without a single Jazz timeout. Will Hardy had the young fella's play through their mistakes, which blew up right in their faces. This was a common culprit in how the Jazz lost games during their tanking years, but it simply cannot carry over in a supposed 'winning' season.

The lucky opponent that gets to face the Jazz is the 9-3 Houston Rockets; both teams will play their second game of the inaugural NBA Cup, where Utah has already fallen 0-1 in their first match against the Dallas Mavericks. The Rockets, on the other hand, are also 0-1. To claim one of the NBA Cup's 'wildcard' spots, both teams will essentially need to be undefeated the rest of their games while also tallying up a high point differential to beat out the others.

Houston has followed the NBA's trend of moving past dying minimalism, rebranding with a new uniform set featuring ketchup and mustard to honor the Hakeem Olajuwon era. Their on-court play has transformed them into a formidable Western Conference contender by focusing on a two-way balance. They post a top-five offensive rating (119.3) and allow 106.5 points a game (4th in the NBA). The Jazz will need to play better than perfect to avoid being locked in the doghouse. It can't be too hard, right? After all, they won't have to be hand-checked by former DPOY Marcus Smart; he's suspended for throwing a mean right hook at the face of Charles Bassey.

Wednesday night saw Franz Wagner be the man of the hour by scoring 12 of his career-high 38 points in the fourth quarter, blowing a kiss to the Utah crowd in his finishing act. The Jazz were vulnerable defending the pick-and-roll, leaving driving lanes open that allowed Wagner to viciously attack the paint.

The Rockets' brand of basketball generares a massive share on physical size and high-volume two-point scoring. Houston averages 12.9 offensive rebounds per game, led by 6'7" Amen Thompson grabbing 3.1 offensive boards a game.

All-Star Alperen Şengün dictates the team's gravity out of the post and elbows, pacing the squad with 21.1 points, 9.4 rebounds and 7.9 assists. He experienced some inefficiency early on in the season, but his footwork and passing vision are something to be cautious about; Utah can't be caught slipping on backdoor cuts.

The clearest path to exploiting Houston is to attack their secondary. Early-season injuries have left Steven Adams and Clint Capela sidelined, forcing them to play deep on their bench, where we'll likely see our old pal Oscar Tshiebwe weather the storm. Utah's second unit is as unpredictable as ever. They can blow a double-digit lead in minutes, or they can also hold down the fort and score 41 bench points against the Orlando Magic. The key is to wear down the non-Şengun minutes by punishing smaller lineups, putting Utah in a strong position to stealan NBA Cup Group Stage matchup.

NEW ORLEANS, LA – NOVEMBER 4: Svi Mykhailiuk #10 of the Utah Jazz drives to the basket during the game against the New Orleans Pelicans on November 4, 2026 at the Smoothie King Center in New Orleans, Louisiana. Truly, the last week has seen some ridiculous stuff from 19-year-old Darryn Peterson.

The Jazz rookie broke out in his latest scoring surge, averaging 23.5 points, 4.0 rebounds and 4.5 assists on 47/38/92 shooting splits over his last three games. I mean, a near 50-40-90 from a 19-year-old rookie is pretty good, right?

Peterson also set a new NBA career high with 26 points against the Timberwolves on November 9, scoring in all four quarters without attempting a single free throw. Alongside his efficient shooting (39.4% from three over the four games), he chipped in on defense with five steals and four blocks total, highlighted by a three-block effort in Dallas.

Utah leaned more on Peterson as its No. 1 option this week after Keyonte's injury, unlocking higher scoring volume while maintaining his playmaking ability. His season scoring average jumped to 16.3 points per game, moving him to No. 4 on the latest NBA.com Kia Rookie Ladder, up from 6th.

Win or lose, Darryn Peterson playing a good brand of basketball is what brings in tickets. If all else fails, the one thing the Utah Jazz do have is their faith in their #2 pick. As long as he's on the court, there's at least one Jazz fan smiling at their TV, because the future has never been this bright for a kid from Canton.

All we've heard about Peterson. Before that, Keyonte George had to carry scoring duties on his back in the midst of Utah's injuries to their frontcourt. It was only recently that Ace Bailey finally began to regain his stride. But the issue is that Utah needs one more guy to step up — someone who makes just enough of a contribution so it doesn't feel like Utah has to fight tooth and nail with .500 teams every single night.

Is it Isaiah Collier? Was it really all it took- a banged-up shoulder for him to have his Thunderstruck moment? I should remind you he hadn't hit a single triple all season before his perfect 4-for-4 night over Miami. That was then followed by another made three-ball against the Magic. In all honesty, all that's needed is for Collier to shoot an amount that at least has his opponent respect his three-point range. The dream is for him to learn how to play more off-ball, while simultaneously addressing his weaknesses in navigating ball screens and managing overall defensive rating metrics

Brice Sensabaugh's role has continually fluctuated through this twelve-game opening stretch. His 23 minutes against the Magic were the most he's played all season. Sure, Brice can shoot the rock. But the harsh truth is he's been unproductive in every other facet of the game. He can't swing the ball; his defense negatively impacts the team, and has shown some really discouraging off-ball lapses. If his shot isn't falling, then that really hurts the Jazz. Having Brice be the 'next man up' means he should be hunting quick-trigger catch-and-shoot threes that could hopefully have a butterfly effect in swinging the game's momentum.

Svi Mykhailiuk has been through an otherworldly shooting funk. I consider myself a Svi truther more than others, but he's had a longer leash compared to other years, despite his 28/21/50 scoring splits—no, that's not a typo. Averaging an honest 16.1 minutes per game, really, shooting above 50% from the field should be the realistic goal.

When is Will Hardy finally going to acknowledge his two-way guys? The expectation was that Kobe Bufkin and Harrison Ingram were going to get their shine after Lauri Markkanen was sidelined, but that moment has yet to come. Instead, Will Hardy opts to reduce his already short-handed rotation, staggering Darryn Peterson and Ace Bailey with the second unit. But when you're 1-11, you really just have to try anything in your arsenal. You don't have time to see if the guys on a cold streak can 'work through their struggles'. Now, it's about who can contribute right this second.

#2858. Why the data center slowdown concern is overblown. Two stocks to buy amid the fear

The demise of data centers has been greatly exaggerated. So says every utility CEO and energy-focused investor we spoke with at the Edison Electric Institute's annual conference in Arizona. Here's why...

There is a lot of misleading, misdirected, or straight-up incorrect information floating around about data centers and the AI buildout. I'm not going to dive into the old water-use story — you can do your own research on that — but rather focus this week on what the insiders know: Some newly elected politicians either don't have the power to stop the buildouts or, if they do, the buildout will likely just continue somewhere else.

Ohio and Georgia are important states to watch with their new incoming political class, but for the sheer importance in size and scope, I'll focus on the energy and data center capital: Texas.

Incoming Governor Gina Hinojosa and incoming Senator James Talarico both campaigned on promises to go after what they believe to be affordability issues around data centers. I say "what they believe to be" because it's unclear how much data centers may raise your electricity bill, if at all.

First and foremost, data centers are largely a state issue, meaning officials elected to federal office have little control over outcomes, at least for now. In Texas, any cancellations or moratoriums on new builds will come from the statehouse in Austin. That's where current Governor Greg Abbott has already, and rather quietly, put his own "pause" on data center permitting. That delay has been underway for a few weeks. Also, in Texas, the Governor has less direct power than in some other states. The power the governor does have largely derives from the authority to appoint those in charge of the all-important permitting process.

In the Lone Star State, that power lies with two agencies: the Public Utility Commission of Texas (PUCT) and the even lesser-known Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ). The PUCT regulates Texas' electricity, telecom, and water industries. It helps oversee growth of anything electricity-grid-related. So if a data center wants to connect to the public utility grid, it needs the commission's approval. Its chair — a pro-growth chap named Thomas Gleeson —is also on the board of Texas' electric grid powerhouse ERCOT and will serve his term with the utility commission through September 2029. Yes, three years from now.

That agency gets involved when a data center or power provider wants to connect to the electricity grid. But what about facilities that are off the grid and have their own dedicated power sources? Texas has less control over what happens then. That said, it does have to authorize certain environmental permits, and that responsibility largely lies with the TCEQ. The TCEQ was "BBI" (my own little term for "boring, but important") until data centers came along. It's not exactly the Alamo, but this obscure group — run by a regulator named Brooke Paup — is largely the "last stand" for permitting on those new builds. Paup was also appointed by Gov. Abbott and serves through next year. In other words, unless she and Gleeson step down or there is some way to oust them, the regulatory environment in Texas may not change much.

The bottom line in this brief foray into the Texas regulatory swamp is to highlight that there are things for investors to consider around the data center/energy world that may not be at the top of the headlines.

Important Note → Be mindful that I am an East Coast-based financial broadcaster and journalist, not an expert on Texas regulation or how the state operates. There may very well be things I don't know, things my sources aren't aware of, or other ways in which the new Governor and state House can assert power. For one, she recently said she may try to freeze electricity rates. Hint: that would be a terrible idea unless you want to damage the growth of badly needed new power sources like wind and solar.

Disclaimer is done. Let's assume I am wrong on Texas and there is a larger push to slow or stop data center growth in the state. Conversations with some pretty smart people lead me to believe that if those facilities aren't built in that state, they will be built in another: Louisiana, Oklahoma, or another state. Sources tell me that Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry and his team are already working the phones, telling companies that his state would love to welcome new business. Same for Oklahoma. Both are gas-rich, have plenty of power, and are only too happy to grab any data center dollars.

Let's acknowledge that not all data centers are going to be built… and never were. There are way too many plans to make them all happen. Some DCs were shelved before the election. Some others may soon be canceled, either for reasons of public pushback or otherwise. It's also not clear if we even need all the planned compute. There will be a time when companies realize they have enough — or at least enough for now — and turn off the spending taps.

Goldman Sachs lays it out in a new note late this week:

"The midterm election results pose only modest risks to AI investment, for now. We expect newly elected Democratic governors in states that account for over a third of planned US data center capacity to impose moratoria on new data centers. However, these will likely only impact projects not yet permitted, leaving the vast majority of planned 2027 construction unaffected. We therefore expect data center construction spending to remain strong next year, though moratoria that are stricter or longer-lasting than we expect pose downside risks."

The firm adds that it still sees spending on AI-related hardware coming in around $1.4 trillion by the end of next year. In other words, the spending boom is going to keep going, at least for now.

Another key point for all you energy- or utility-focused investors out there: While data centers are one huge driver of electricity demand, they aren't the only one. Electric cars, reshoring manufacturing, and the "electrification of everything" will also grow the need for more gigawatts. This is why we asked every utility CEO we spoke with at the EEI conference if they saw any slowdown in their internal electricity growth estimates. All gave the same answer: no. Not one hinted at a downturn. Not Exelon's Calvin Butler. Not Entergy's Drew Marsh. And not Drew Maloney, head of EEI. Of course, power demand is about more than just data centers, but it's still important for investors to hear it from the top.

Side note: Many of you think I'm wrong about my "hot take" on data centers and the election. If you missed last week's Power Insider, this is how you responded to my X poll on the election and energy.

So… why am I writing this week about AI and data centers? Because, as I've said before and will keep saying, AI and data centers are just energy stories in disguise. If they grow, energy demand grows. Demand for solar, wind, and natural gas grows. The economy grows. Because, after all, energy is the economy.

Oh, and in the past week, I've flown from Newark to Rome to Chicago to Phoenix and back to Newark. My brain may be a little mushy, so bear with me.

So now that my big take on data centers and regulation is over, let's get granular. What energy stocks do you buy right now?

Let's get the "1-2-3" on six stocks: one in solar, two in power, and three in oil and gas.

One: William Blair is upgrading First Solar (FSLR) to Outperform. The election result is part of the story. Analyst Jed Dorsheimer and his team believe the new Congress and certain state governorships will benefit local support for utility-scale solar, and that First Solar is best-positioned, though he does caution investors that it may take "some time to show up in the bookings." He adds that Texas is the largest builder of solar, so the shift in state-level leadership is a big deal. Fair warning, however: upgrades do not mean the stock will go higher. Deutsche Bank upgraded FSLR back in early July — an upgrade I wrote about — and the stock has fallen 22% since.

Two: Staying with the macro power theme, Barclays' Nicholas Campanella is particularly optimistic on two utility names. He tells Power Insider that he "really likes NiSource (NI) and Xcel Energy (XEL) right now." It has to do in part with what I just wrote about above. Campanella says the political outcomes in states such as Texas and Georgia may drive investors into utilities in other areas, particularly those with "more stable and established data center policies in place." That would fit with Indiana-based NiSource and Minnesota-based Xcel. While Minnesota may have a Democratic tilt, the company operates in eight different states. It is also working with Alphabet (GOOGL) on a massive new data center, and CEO Bob Frenzel told us that he is confident they can successfully navigate it all.

Three: Now to the "3"… three picks on what powers some of the power: oil and natural gas.

In an interview on CNBC, Bernstein analyst Bob Brackett says he likes ExxonMobil (XOM), calling it a great "barbell" play between natural gas and refined products. Brackett — a former Exxon employee — also believes it's a relatively "safe" stock to own right now in the volatile and headline-driven world of energy. Brackett has an Outperform rating and $186 target on the stock, giving it about another 10% upside from here.

Brackett also likes the lesser-known Expand Energy (EXE). If you're not familiar with Expand Energy, don't worry; it's still relatively new. The company is the combination of Chesapeake Energy and Southwestern Energy. It's a natural gas behemoth, and Brackett says the gas story is a "great long-term story" that's trading below a "midcycle price," and Expand fits right into it. Brackett also notes that Expand has a new CEO. He has a $139 target on the stock, making him one of the more bullish analysts on the Street. Expand has a median price target of $123.54.

Meanwhile, the chart looks tasty on another energy name: Cheniere Energy (LNG). Piper Sandler technical guru Craig Johnson likes the stock, and while he isn't a fundamental energy analyst, he was one of the few to upgrade the entire sector back in February when the Iran war began. Cheniere — along with Venture Global (VG) — is a pure play on why I was just in Europe: the worsening European energy crisis. Cheniere exports massive quantities of U.S. natural gas to customers in Europe, Asia, and other parts of the world. Johnson likes Cheniere in part because it recently broke out of a "very big base," and he wants to buy stocks that are on the upswing.

Cheniere is a well-run, rather quiet company with a fantastic CEO in longtime energy executive Jack Fusco. Jack has forgotten more about running an energy business than most will know, and the company continues to expand production. As I've pointed out for over four years, without American natural gas, Europe would be in dire straits and cutting demand even more than it already is. It's not hyperbole to say that American natural gas is — literally — keeping the lights and the heat on across Germany, Italy, and other countries.

Remember this incredible stat: just one tanker of LNG can produce a year's worth of power for over 100,000 average-sized European homes. Next year, Europe is still planning to stop buying LNG from Russia. That means demand for American natural gas is only going one way: up.

This week's Inside Line is with Amrita Sen, founder and director of market intelligence at Energy Aspects. Amrita is one of the smartest and most well-connected energy experts in the world, and she took a few minutes from her constant travel to answer a couple of questions.

This week's Take A Look is a little outside the norm, but an important interview nonetheless. It's with the U.S. Ambassador to Italy, business magnate, and former star of CNBC's Billion Dollar Buyer, Tilman Fertitta. In a wide-ranging interview from the ambassador's residence in Rome, we talked about that day's stock market crash, business, the consumer, and, of course, energy. Full disclosure: Tilman and I have been friends for over 15 years.

This Washington Post article was published a few days after the election. The underlying research from Harvard University and the University of Pennsylvania that it was based on is here and is worth a read. The study came out on October 9 (and yes, I missed it as well).

Not blaming data centers for higher energy costs is definitely random, but interesting!

#2857. Michigan Wolverines arrive in Eugene before FAA grounding

It's shaping up to be another big Saturday in college football, including at Autzen Stadium when the No. 16 Oregon Ducks host the Michigan Wolverines.

As of now, that game is scheduled to proceed with kickoff at 12:30 p.m. PST, despite some of the travel issues other programs are facing across the country. The FAA issued a nationwide ground stop on Friday, causing trouble for some teams trying to reach their game-day locations, some of which, because of conference realignment, are multiple states apart.

Michigan won't be affected, however, as the team arrived in Eugene on Thursday before the nationwide grounding of flights, according to multiple reports. There has not been an announcement regarding a delay of the game on Saturday in Eugene.

The FAA is investigating a communication issue that is impacting all Air Route Traffic Control Centers, leaving the status of several games in limbo around the country. According to The Athletic, Utah, Texas, Tennessee, Ole Miss, Arizona State and Oregon State are among the teams that have seen their travel impacted ahead of Saturday's slate of games.

The broadcast of this game may be impacted by the ground stop. Tim Brando and the FS1 crew are in Los Angeles, slated to cover the UCLA vs. Illinois game on Friday night. It is currently unclear if they will be able to make it to Eugene for a 12:30 p.m. PT kick on Saturday.

There is no word on how long the ground stop will take. If the delay lasts long enough, it could impact Michigan's ability to return home after its game against the Ducks.

Oregon and Michigan are still scheduled to play at 12:30 p.m. PST at Autzen Stadium on Saturday. The game will be broadcast live on FS1.

#2856. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.
